CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 15
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what each remaining condition removed

```text
  Each condition is best understood by the CONSTRAINT it removed.

  CONSTRAINT BEFORE                    CONDITION           WHAT IS NOW POSSIBLE
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  A mill must stand on falling      >  COAL            >  A mill may stand where
  water; its size is limited by        (abundant)         labour, materials and
  the flow of that stream                                 transport are best

  Machines must be made of wood     >  IRON            >  Machines that make
  or scarce metal; they break          (abundant)         machines; durable
  and cannot bear force                                   precision parts

  A machine costs more than a       >  CAPITAL         >  Fixed investment made
  producer earns in years; no          (trade,            BEFORE any return
  one can buy it                       colonies,          arrives
                                       plunder)

  Producing more than the local     >  MARKETS         >  Producing at a scale
  market can absorb is a loss          (home +            that justifies the
                                       overseas +         machine in the first
                                       colonies)          place

  Investment can be seized;         >  LEGAL AND       >  A long-horizon
  contracts cannot be enforced;        POLITICAL          investment becomes a
  policy is arbitrary                  ORDER              rational act

  The cost advantage is lost in     >  TRANSPORT       >  The lower unit cost
  carriage; distant markets are        (canals,           SURVIVES the journey
  unreachable                          roads, ships,
                                       later rails)

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  READ THE RIGHT-HAND COLUMN DOWNWARDS: it is the industrial
  economy, assembled from six removals of six constraints.
```

*A condition that has not been converted into a removed constraint is still a list item. This visual is the conversion, and it is the form in which these six conditions should be carried into an examination hall.*

### 1. Coal: what it actually removed

✅ **Fact:** England had **coal and iron in abundance**, and the recorded mechanism for coal is precise: coal **removed the ceiling that water power imposed on mill siting and scale**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** unpack that and it becomes the strongest single sentence available on this condition. A water-powered mill must be built where a stream falls; the stream's flow sets the maximum power available, and therefore the maximum size of the enterprise; and the stream may freeze, flood or dry. A coal-fired mill can be built wherever coal can be delivered, can be enlarged by burning more coal, and does not depend on weather. Coal converts power from a *site-specific natural endowment* into a *purchasable input*. That is the difference between an industry that can be planned and one that must be found.

✅ **Fact:** The recorded limitation is equally important and is the sufficiency test in one line: **resources alone explain nothing - other regions had coal and did not industrialise first.**

### 2. Iron: the condition that makes machinery a system

✅ **Fact:** The sector table records that **coal and iron together supported machinery production**, with the effect that **machine-building expands**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytically interesting property of iron is recursive. Iron makes machines; machines make iron more cheaply; cheaper iron makes more machines. A wooden machine wears, warps and cannot transmit large forces; an iron one can, and can be made to a repeatable specification. Once machine parts can be made by machines, the economy has acquired something it did not previously possess: a *capital-goods sector*, an industry whose customers are other industries. That is why coal and iron belong together in an answer and why separating them loses the argument.

### 3. Capital: what it financed, and the honest treatment of its colonial component

✅ **Fact:** The sources link **expanding trade, colonies and plundered wealth** to capital formation, and record the mechanism: it **made fixed investment in mills and engines financeable before returns arrived**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "before returns arrived" is doing the work. A hand tool pays for itself almost immediately. A mill and an engine must be paid for years before the first surplus appears, and somebody must be willing and able to carry that gap. An economy can contain enormous wealth and still not industrialise if that wealth is held in forms that cannot or will not accept a long, risky, illiquid commitment. What commercial and colonial profit supplied was not merely money but money already accustomed to distant, delayed and risky returns.

✅ **Fact:** The recorded caution is explicit: **colonial profit is one source among several; do not make empire the single cause of British industrialisation.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this caution is examinable in both directions, and stating both sides is what a "critically examine" directive rewards. Writing that empire caused British industrialisation overstates a contribution the sources record as one among several. Writing that empire was incidental understates an account that explicitly names colonies and plundered wealth as sources of investible capital and colonies as a widener of demand. The defensible position sits between: colonial wealth and colonial demand were substantial contributors to a process they did not by themselves produce.

### 4. Markets: the condition that makes scale rational

✅ **Fact:** **Overseas trade and colonies widened demand**, and the mechanism is recorded as justifying production **far beyond local need - the precondition for scale**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the condition candidates most often treat as an effect rather than a cause, and the ordering matters. A machine is only worth buying if the output can be sold. A producer who can sell a hundred pieces will not buy a machine that makes ten thousand. Therefore a large accessible market is not a consequence of mechanisation; it is a *precondition* of the decision to mechanise. That reversal is worth an explicit sentence in any "why Britain" answer.

✅ **Fact:** The recorded caution: **domestic demand also mattered; do not reduce it to exports.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the caution protects against a common and attractive error - treating British industrialisation as an export phenomenon driven entirely by empire. A home market that is unified, commercially integrated and populous enough to absorb standardised goods is itself an asset, and it is the asset that Lesson 8 shows transport deepening.

### 5. Institutions: what "stability" means, stated without sentiment

✅ **Fact:** The **post-seventeenth-century English state was more favourable to commerce**, and the recorded mechanism is that it **lowered the risk of expropriation and enforced contracts**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that mechanism is narrower and more useful than the vague word "stability". What a person contemplating a fifteen-year investment needs is not tranquillity but predictability: confidence that the asset will not be seized, that an agreement will be enforceable against a defaulting partner, and that the rules will not be rewritten arbitrarily. Note that this is a claim about the *security of commercial expectations*, not a claim about liberty, democracy or good government, none of which the sources assert for this period.

✅ **Fact:** The recorded caution is a single decisive word: **"stability" is comparative, not absolute.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison is with contemporaries, and the eighteenth century was not peaceful. An answer that writes "England enjoyed peace and stability while Europe was in turmoil" asserts more than the evidence supports; an answer that writes "commercial expectations in England were comparatively more secure, which lowered the effective risk of long-horizon industrial investment" asserts exactly what it can defend.

### 6. Transport as a precondition, not only a consequence

✅ **Fact:** The sector table records **railways, canals, macadam roads and steam boats** together, with the effect that **raw materials and finished goods move faster**. The evidence bank adds the analytical form of the same point: transport **lowers delivered cost**, so that **the cost advantage survives the journey to distant markets**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "survives the journey" is the examinable idea and it deserves one sentence in any answer that mentions transport. A producer whose unit cost is lower at the factory gate has gained nothing if carriage charges erase the difference before the goods reach a buyer. Cheap transport is therefore not merely a convenience; it is what converts a *production* advantage into a *market* advantage.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that canals, macadam roads and coastal or ocean shipping belong to the *preconditions*, while the railway belongs to the *deepening* phase. The dated marker for the first passenger railway is 1830, decades after the first textile machinery. This ordering is the trap Lesson 8 addresses, and it should be kept straight from the outset: Britain did not industrialise because of railways; railways were the transport technology that a already-industrialising economy generated and then used to integrate a national market.

### 7. The whole set, with the removal test applied

| Condition | What it removed | Sufficiency test - present elsewhere without industrialisation? | Recorded limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agricultural surplus | The subsistence constraint on town growth | ⚠️ Yes | ✅ Regionally uneven |
| Released labour | The alternative to accepting factory discipline | ⚠️ Yes | ✅ Long and contested, not a single event |
| Coal | The water-power ceiling on siting and scale | ⚠️ Yes - other regions had coal | ✅ Resources alone explain nothing |
| Iron | The material limit on machine-building | ⚠️ Yes | ⚠️ Useless without coal for smelting and power |
| Capital | The impossibility of pre-return investment | ⚠️ Yes | ✅ Colonial profit is one source among several |
| Markets | The pointlessness of producing beyond local need | ⚠️ Yes | ✅ Domestic demand also mattered |
| Institutions | The risk of expropriation and unenforceable contracts | ⚠️ Yes | ✅ Stability is comparative, not absolute |
| Transport | The loss of the cost advantage in carriage | ⚠️ Yes | ⚠️ Railways came after the first wave, not before |

✅ **Fact:** The verdict this table supports is supplied by the sources themselves: **not one cause but a conjunction; remove any single element and the sequence slows, but remove secure markets and cheap coal together and it does not begin.**

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The account is circular. It says markets were needed to justify machines, and that machines produced the goods that created markets; that capital was needed for mills, and that mills produced the profits that became capital. Every condition turns out to be partly an effect of the process it is supposed to explain, which means the explanation has no independent starting point.

**Reply:** The circularity is real and is a property of the historical process rather than a defect in the account, but it can be broken at a specific point, and naming that point is what a strong answer does. The market that justified the first textile machine was not created by that machine: it already existed as overseas trade, colonial demand and a commercially integrated home market. The capital that financed the first mill was not generated by that mill: it came from trade, colonies and accumulated commercial wealth. In other words, the *first* round of each loop was supplied from outside the industrial process, by a commercial and colonial economy that already existed. After the first round the loops become self-reinforcing, and from that point the circularity is a description of how industrialisation sustained itself rather than a flaw in the explanation of how it started.

**Residual:** Where this evidence base leaves the argument open is on the relative size of the external contributions that started each loop - how much of the initial investible capital was colonial and how much domestic-commercial, and how much of the initial demand was overseas and how much home. The sources name both components on both sides and apportion neither, and this session does not supply an apportionment it cannot evidence.

### UPSC application

This lesson, with Lesson 3, is the complete apparatus for the highest-frequency framing in the topic. Its distinctive asset is the removed-constraint form: a candidate who writes "coal removed the ceiling that water power imposed on mill siting and scale" has written something an examiner will remember, and a candidate who writes "England was rich in coal" has not. For "why England first" at 250 words, use six conditions with one removed constraint each, attach two recorded limitations, and close with the markets-and-coal ranking. For an "industrialisation and imperialism" question, the capital caution in section 3 is the sentence that keeps the answer on the right side of overstatement. For a question on the role of the state, the institutional mechanism in section 5 pairs with Lesson 12 to give a before-and-after argument about what the state did for industry and what industry then forced the state to do.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing coal and iron as possessions without naming the constraints each removed.
- Separating iron from coal, and thereby losing the capital-goods argument.
- Making empire the single cause of British industrialisation, against an explicit recorded caution.
- Reducing demand to exports and omitting the home market, against an explicit recorded caution.
- Treating "political stability" as tranquillity or good government rather than as the security of commercial expectations.
- Presenting the railway as a cause of British industrialisation rather than as its later instrument.
- Failing to answer the "others had it too" objection, which every single condition invites.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Coal's mechanism: removed the ceiling that water power imposed on mill siting and scale.
2. ✅ Coal's limitation: resources alone explain nothing; other regions had coal and did not industrialise first.
3. ✅ Coal and iron together supported machinery production; machine-building expands.
4. ⚠️ Iron's recursive property: iron makes machines, machines make iron cheaper, and a capital-goods sector appears.
5. ✅ Capital's mechanism: made fixed investment in mills and engines financeable before returns arrived.
6. ✅ Capital's caution: colonial profit is one source among several; empire is not the single cause.
7. ✅ Markets' mechanism: justified producing far beyond local need - the precondition for scale.
8. ✅ Markets' caution: domestic demand also mattered.
9. ✅ Institutions' mechanism: lowered the risk of expropriation and enforced contracts.
10. ✅ Institutions' caution: stability is comparative, not absolute.
11. ✅ Transport's mechanism: lowered delivered cost, so the cost advantage survived the journey.
12. ⚠️ Canals, macadam roads and shipping are preconditions; the railway of 1830 is a deepening instrument.
13. ✅ Verdict: a conjunction, not a cause; remove one element and it slows, remove secure markets and cheap coal together and it does not begin.
14. ⚠️ The circularity is broken at the first round - the initial market and the initial capital came from the pre-existing commercial and colonial economy.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain in one paragraph why cheap transport should be counted among the causes of industrial take-off and not only among its effects, and state the limitation that must accompany the claim.

**Model answer:** A producer who mechanises gains a lower cost per unit at the factory gate, but that gain is realised only if the goods reach a buyer with the advantage intact; if carriage charges are high enough, the cheaper good arrives dearer than a local hand-made one and the machine was not worth buying. Cheap transport therefore does not merely distribute the benefits of mechanisation after the fact, it is part of what makes mechanisation a rational investment in the first place, because it is what allows a production advantage to survive the journey and become a market advantage. In Britain, canals, macadam roads and coastal and ocean shipping supplied that condition alongside the other five, and the sources record them together with railways as the transport change that moved raw materials and finished goods faster. The limitation is chronological and must be stated: the passenger railway is dated 1830, decades after the first textile machinery, so railways cannot be offered as a cause of the initial take-off. They are the transport technology that an already-industrialising economy generated and then used to integrate a national market, which is precisely why the railway is treated separately and by country type rather than as one more British advantage.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the removal of one constraint as an explanation of the whole outcome. Every condition in this set removes a genuine constraint and none is sufficient, which is why an answer must state what each removed and then say explicitly that the outcome required all of them together in one economy at one time.

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** The conditions are now complete, and they explain why an economy could mechanise. They do not explain why it mechanised *cotton cloth* rather than iron, ships, furniture, glass or anything else. The next lesson answers that, and the answer turns out to explain the shape of the entire first phase.

## Lesson 5 - Cotton first: why mechanisation began in a cheap consumer good

Progress: 5/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the spinning jenny, water frame and power loom; the spinning-weaving imbalance; the cotton gin and the transatlantic raw-material dependence; why the leading sector was textiles and what that determined

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's sector table on textiles and raw-cotton processing, its dated inventions from 1764 to 1793, the must-know facts attaching each machine to its inventor, and the evidence unit that treats textile mechanisation as step one of the transmissible industrial mechanism
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 15
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the bottleneck chase, and where it ended

```text
  A textile industry has three stages. Mechanise one and the
  others become the constraint. The first phase of the Industrial
  Revolution is the record of that constraint moving.

  STAGE:     CLEAN & PREPARE  ->   SPIN (fibre to yarn)  ->  WEAVE (yarn to cloth)

  STEP 0     hand-cleaned          hand spinning-wheel       handloom
  (before)   slow                  slow                      slow
             ---------------------------------------------------------
             the whole chain is slow and BALANCED: no stage is
             conspicuously holding up the others

  STEP 1     hand-cleaned      ->  SPINNING JENNY 1764   ->  handloom
                                   multiple spindles         UNCHANGED
             ---------------------------------------------------------
             SPINNING LEAPS AHEAD. Yarn accumulates faster than
             weavers can use it. THE BOTTLENECK MOVES TO WEAVING.

  STEP 2     hand-cleaned      ->  WATER FRAME 1769      ->  handloom
                                   stronger yarn, powered    STILL BEHIND
             ---------------------------------------------------------
             The imbalance widens. Weaving is now the constraint on
             the whole industry.

  STEP 3     hand-cleaned      ->  jenny + water frame   ->  POWER LOOM 1785
             STILL SLOW           fast                       fast
             ---------------------------------------------------------
             Weaving catches up. Now BOTH spinning and weaving are
             mechanised - and the constraint jumps BACKWARDS to the
             supply of clean raw fibre.

  STEP 4     COTTON GIN 1793   ->  jenny + water frame   ->  power loom
             (Whitney, USA)        fast                      fast
             fibre separation
             sharply faster
             ---------------------------------------------------------
             THE CHAIN IS NOW MECHANISED END TO END - and its raw
             material comes from across an ocean.
```

*The diagram is the argument. Mechanisation is not a shower of unrelated inventions; it is a sequence in which each machine creates the problem that the next machine solves. Reproduce this sequence in an answer and the invention list becomes a mechanism.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Why did the first machines appear in cloth-making? The intuitive answers are wrong in instructive ways. It was not because cloth was the most valuable product, nor because textiles were technically the easiest thing to mechanise, nor because Britain had some unique aptitude for weaving.

The usable answer has three parts, and each is a general principle that can be reused for any leading-sector question. First, cloth is a **universal necessity**: everybody needs it, everybody replaces it, and demand does not depend on persuading anyone to want something new. Second, the work is **repetitive and decomposable**: spinning and weaving consist of a small number of motions repeated indefinitely, which is precisely the kind of task a machine can take over. Third, the industry was **already organised commercially** through the putting-out system, so a merchant class with capital, market knowledge and an existing distribution network was already in place and already frustrated by the limits of household production.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** put the three together and cotton is not a surprising choice but an overdetermined one: large and price-sensitive demand, mechanisable motions, and an existing commercial organisation with a motive to raise output. A candidate who states those three criteria has a portable tool - the same three explain why later industrialisations also began in textiles, which is the observation Lesson 14 relies on.

### 2. The four machines, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** The sources record the textile sector's machines and their effect as follows:

| Year | Machine | Inventor | ✅ Recorded effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1764 | Spinning jenny | James Hargreaves | Faster spinning in textiles |
| 1769 | Water frame | Richard Arkwright | Mechanisation deepens |
| 1785 | Power loom | Edmund Cartwright | Weaving accelerates |
| 1793 | Cotton gin | Eli Whitney (United States) | Raw-cotton processing speeds up sharply; fibre separation becomes much faster |

✅ **Fact:** The sector-level effect recorded for the spinning jenny, water frame and power loom together is that **cotton-textile output rises sharply**.

✅ **Fact:** The must-know list ties each name to its machine and states that **textiles were the earliest leading sector**.

### 3. The imbalance argument, which is what the dates are for

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the twenty-one years between the spinning jenny of 1764 and the power loom of 1785 are the most instructive interval in the topic, and almost no candidate uses them. During that period spinning was mechanised and weaving was not. The industry therefore ran with one stage transformed and the next stage unchanged, which produces a specific and predictable set of consequences.

| Consequence of the 1764-1785 imbalance | ⚠️ Why it follows |
|---|---|
| Yarn becomes relatively abundant | Mechanised spinning outruns hand weaving |
| Pressure to mechanise weaving intensifies | The unmechanised stage becomes the visible constraint on profit |
| Handloom weaving is temporarily *favoured* | More yarn at lower cost means more work for weavers, before the power loom arrives |
| The later displacement is therefore severe | A trade that had expanded on cheap yarn is then undercut by the machine that follows |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that fourth row is worth an explicit sentence in any answer about social consequences, and it disciplines a common oversimplification. Mechanisation did not uniformly and immediately destroy handwork. It first *expanded* one kind of handwork by supplying its input more cheaply, and then destroyed it when the machine reached that stage too. Displacement in this industry was sequential, not simultaneous - and a trade that had recently grown was the one that collapsed.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Between 1764 and 1785 mechanisation ran ahead of itself, expanding hand weaving on cheap machine yarn before the power loom removed it - which is why handloom displacement was delayed, then abrupt.

### 4. The cotton gin, and the point that is usually missed

✅ **Fact:** The cotton gin is recorded as **Eli Whitney's, 1793**, in the **United States**, and the sources explicitly forbid asserting it as a British invention.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytically important feature is not the device but its location. Britain's mechanised cotton industry did not grow its own raw material. Its decisive raw-fibre bottleneck was cleared by a machine invented in the economy that supplied the fibre, on the other side of an ocean. Three consequences follow, and each is usable.

1. ⚠️ The leading sector of the first industrial revolution was, from the beginning, **dependent on an intercontinental raw-material supply** - which means the British industrial economy was never a self-contained national system.
2. ⚠️ That dependence is one concrete mechanism behind the sources' general claim that industrialisation intensified the **search for raw materials**, developed in Lesson 15. It is better than the general claim because it names a specific commodity, a specific stage and a specific date.
3. ⚠️ Technical improvement in one economy raised the demand for a plantation-produced commodity in another. This session does not narrate the American plantation system, which belongs to other owners, but the connection should be stated rather than silently omitted, because an answer on the global effects of industrialisation that mentions only markets and omits raw-material sourcing has described half the relationship.

### 5. What the leading sector determined

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the choice of cotton as the leading sector shaped the whole first phase, and this is the paragraph that converts the lesson from description into analysis.

| Because the leading sector was cotton textiles | The consequence for the first phase |
|---|---|
| The product was cheap, light and standardisable | Distant markets were reachable and worth supplying, which is why transport mattered so much |
| The raw material was imported | The industry's external dependence was structural from the outset |
| The machines were not enormous at first | Early mills could use water power, which delayed but did not remove the need for steam |
| The work required attendance and dexterity more than strength | Women and children were economically usable, which is why Lesson 10 is a lesson and not a footnote |
| The finished good competed directly with hand-made cloth everywhere | The competitive pressure was exported wherever the cloth was sold, which is the opening of the question Lesson 15 hands over |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth row deserves emphasis because it is the hinge between the technical and the social halves of this topic. A leading sector that had required great physical strength would have produced a different workforce and therefore a different social history. Cotton did not require it, and the labour consequences follow directly.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Presenting textile mechanisation as a self-propelling chain of bottlenecks is too neat. Inventions are not produced on demand; the power loom did not appear because weaving had become the constraint, it appeared because an individual happened to devise it. The account substitutes an economic logic for the contingency of invention, and the twenty-one-year gap between 1764 and 1785 is itself evidence that no mechanism guaranteed the next step.

**Reply:** The objection is right that demand does not manufacture inventions and wrong to conclude that the sequence is therefore arbitrary. The bottleneck argument does not claim that a constraint produces a device; it claims that a constraint determines where the reward for a device is concentrated, and therefore where effort and investment are directed and where a successful device will be adopted rapidly rather than ignored. The twenty-one-year gap supports rather than undermines that reading: it shows exactly that the solution was not automatic, while the speed with which power weaving was taken up once available shows that the reward had indeed accumulated at that stage. Invention is contingent; adoption is structured.

**Residual:** What this evidence base does not hold is any measure of the imbalance - how much yarn accumulated, how far weaving wages rose before the power loom and how fast they fell afterwards, or how quickly power weaving was adopted. The imbalance argument is therefore taught here as a qualitative sequence anchored to two dates, and any quantitative claim about it would be unsupported.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the mechanism paragraph in almost every answer on this topic, and it is the first of the five steps in the evidence unit that a question on England's effect on handicrafts elsewhere requires. Its highest-value move is converting the invention list into the bottleneck sequence: name the four machines with dates and inventors, then show in one sentence that each created the constraint the next solved. For a question on social consequences, use the delayed-then-abrupt displacement point, which distinguishes a good answer from a generic one. For a question about global effects, use the cotton gin's location to make the raw-material dependence concrete instead of asserting it in the abstract. Never spend more than three or four lines on the machines themselves; the sequence is the content, the devices are the evidence.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Listing the machines without the sequence, which loses the mechanism and keeps only the trivia.
- Attaching the wrong inventor to a machine; the four pairings are the most commonly tested factual items in this topic.
- Asserting the cotton gin as British, against an explicit recorded control.
- Claiming that mechanisation destroyed handwork immediately and uniformly, when the sequence shows expansion before displacement.
- Treating textiles as the leading sector by accident, instead of deriving it from demand, decomposability and existing commercial organisation.
- Supplying output, price or employment figures for the cotton industry, none of which this evidence base holds.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1764 spinning jenny, James Hargreaves - faster spinning.
2. ✅ 1769 water frame, Richard Arkwright - mechanisation deepens.
3. ✅ 1785 power loom, Edmund Cartwright - weaving accelerates.
4. ✅ 1793 cotton gin, Eli Whitney, United States - fibre separation becomes much faster.
5. ✅ Sector effect: cotton-textile output rises sharply; textiles were the earliest leading sector.
6. ⚠️ Three criteria for a leading sector: universal price-sensitive demand; repetitive decomposable work; an existing commercial organisation with capital and distribution.
7. ⚠️ The bottleneck chase: jenny and water frame move the constraint to weaving; the power loom moves it back to raw-fibre supply; the gin clears it.
8. ⚠️ 1764-1785 imbalance: cheap machine yarn temporarily expanded hand weaving, which is why its later displacement was abrupt.
9. ⚠️ The gin's location makes the industry's intercontinental raw-material dependence structural, not incidental.
10. ⚠️ Cheap, light, standardised cloth is what made distant markets worth supplying - the link to transport and to Lesson 15.
11. ⚠️ Cotton work needed attendance and dexterity more than strength, which made women's and children's labour economically usable.
12. ⚠️ No cotton-industry output, price, wage or employment figures are available from this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show, using only the four dated textile machines, that the first phase of mechanisation was a sequence rather than a collection - and state what the sequence implies about the industry's external dependence.

**Model answer:** The spinning jenny of 1764 and the water frame of 1769 mechanised spinning while weaving remained a hand process, so yarn accumulated faster than weavers could absorb it and the constraint on the industry moved to weaving; Cartwright's power loom of 1785 removed that constraint, after which both spinning and weaving were mechanised and the binding limit moved backwards to the supply of clean raw fibre; Whitney's cotton gin of 1793 cleared that limit by making fibre separation much faster. Each machine therefore created the problem the next one solved, which is why the four devices constitute a sequence rather than a list of unrelated inventions. The sequence also settles the question of dependence: the machine that cleared the raw-material bottleneck was invented in the United States, not in Britain, and the fibre itself was imported, so the leading sector of the first industrial revolution relied from the outset on an intercontinental supply chain. That is a concrete instance of the general claim that industrialisation intensified the search for raw materials, and it is stronger than the general claim because it names the commodity, the stage and the date.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because a constraint existed, the machine that removed it was bound to appear. The twenty-one-year gap between 1764 and 1785 shows that invention was not produced on demand; what the constraint determined was where the reward for a successful device accumulated, and therefore how rapidly it was adopted once someone had devised it.

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** Every machine so far has needed power, and until now that power came from falling water - which meant it came with a place attached. The next lesson examines what Watt's improvements actually removed, and why an answer that calls the steam engine "important" without saying what it made possible has said nothing.

## Lesson 6 - Steam: what Watt's engine removed, and the coal-iron-machine loop

Progress: 6/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the water-power constraint; Watt's improvements rather than invention; power as a purchasable input; the self-reinforcing loop between coal, iron and machinery; why steam is necessary but not initiating

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's energy row recording that Watt's steam engine took production beyond the limits of muscle and water power, its metals row on coal and iron supporting machinery production, and the factual-risk control forbidding attribution of the engine's invention solely to Watt
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 15
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: power before and after, and the loop that follows

```text
  BEFORE STEAM                          AFTER STEAM
  ------------------------------        ------------------------------
  POWER IS A PLACE                      POWER IS A PURCHASE
  A mill must be built where a          A mill may be built where
  stream falls fast enough.             coal can be delivered.

  SCALE IS SET BY THE STREAM            SCALE IS SET BY INVESTMENT
  You cannot make the river             You can install a bigger
  larger.                               engine and burn more coal.

  SUPPLY IS SEASONAL                    SUPPLY IS CONTINUOUS
  Freeze, flood or drought              Coal is stored, carried and
  stops production.                     burned when required.

  SITES ARE SCARCE AND SCATTERED        SITES CLUSTER
  Mills spread thinly along             Mills concentrate near coal,
  water courses.                        labour and transport -> TOWNS


  AND THEN THE LOOP CLOSES:

            +-----------------------------------+
            |                                   |
            v                                   |
      COAL is mined and burned                  |
            |                                   |
            v                                   |
      STEAM ENGINES drive pumps, mills          |
      and (later) locomotives                   |
            |                                   |
            v                                   |
      more IRON can be produced and worked      |
            |                                   |
            v                                   |
      better MACHINES - including better        |
      engines and better mining equipment       |
            |                                   |
            +-----------------------------------+
              which raises coal output again

   Each element makes the others cheaper. This loop, not any
   single device, is what makes the transformation self-sustaining.
```

*The left-to-right comparison explains what steam removed. The loop underneath explains why the change did not stop. An answer that has both has the whole energy argument.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Before steam, power was not something you bought; it was something you found. Muscle, animals, wind and falling water were the options, and each carried a constraint that no amount of money could lift. A stream's flow cannot be increased. A windmill cannot be run on a calm day. A horse cannot be made to pull harder by paying it more.

This is why the water frame, despite being a genuine advance, did not by itself produce an industrial economy: it tied production to a site chosen by geology rather than by economics. Such sites are limited in number, scattered along valleys, often far from coal, from labour and from ports, and shared with other users of the same water.

What Watt's improvements did was convert power into a *purchasable input*. Once power comes from burning a fuel, the amount of power available is a function of how much fuel you buy, and the place where you produce is a function of where you can have the fuel delivered. Both constraints move from nature to commerce. That single conversion is the content of this lesson.

### 2. The recorded claim, stated exactly and attributed correctly

✅ **Fact:** The sources record the energy change as **Watt's steam engine**, with the effect that **production leaves the limits of muscle and water power**.

✅ **Fact:** The attribution control is explicit and must be respected: **do not attribute the steam engine's invention solely to Watt; his improvements were the decisive breakthrough for industrial use.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the correct formulation is that Watt converted an existing device into an industrially viable prime mover - efficient enough, reliable enough and general enough in application to be worth installing in a manufacturing enterprise rather than only at a mine. The examinable difference is between a device that exists and a device that is worth buying, and the second is an economic threshold rather than a technical one.

✅ **Fact:** The evidence unit for the transmissible mechanism records the consequence in the form most useful for an answer: **steam removes the siting and scale limit**, so that **output can expand without waiting for water sites, and unit costs keep falling.**

### 3. Why "unit costs keep falling" is the operative phrase

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the phrase to carry into an examination, because it links the energy lesson to every later lesson in the session. A one-off fall in cost is an event; a *continuing* fall in cost is a process, and only a process produces the consequences this topic is about.

```text
  steam removes the ceiling on mill size
              |
              v
  larger runs of identical output
              |
              v
  fixed cost of the machine spread over more units
              |
              v
  unit cost falls -> and falls again as scale grows
              |
   +----------+----------+----------------+
   |                     |                |
   v                     v                v
  machine-made      producers who         distant markets
  cloth undercuts   cannot mechanise      become worth
  hand-made cloth   are squeezed          supplying
  AT HOME           progressively         (Lesson 8)
   |                     |                |
   +----------+----------+----------------+
              |
              v
   the same pressure travels wherever the cloth is sold
              (Lesson 15 - and the hand-off)
```

### 4. The loop: why coal, iron and machinery must be taught together

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sources record coal and iron together as supporting machinery production, with machine-building expanding as a result. Steam completes that relationship into a loop, and the loop is the reason the transformation was self-sustaining rather than a single burst.

| Link in the loop | ✅ or ⚠️ | What it contributes |
|---|---|---|
| Coal is mined and burned | ✅ England had coal in abundance | The fuel base |
| Steam engines are installed | ✅ Watt's engine ends the muscle-and-water limit | Power independent of site and season |
| Engines make mining and smelting more productive | ⚠️ Inference joining the recorded coal-iron-machinery relation | More coal and more iron |
| More iron means better machines | ✅ Coal and iron support machinery production | Durable, precise, force-bearing machines |
| Better machines include better engines | ⚠️ Inference | The loop closes and repeats |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest labelling matters here. Two links are direct source claims and two are inferences that join them. An answer may state the loop confidently provided it presents the connective steps as reasoning rather than as recorded fact, which is exactly the fact-and-inference discipline this session runs on.

### 5. Steam's place in the causal order: necessary, but not initiating

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is a strong temptation, encouraged by popular accounts, to make the steam engine the origin of the whole process. The sources block it twice.

✅ **Fact:** The recorded caution states that the steam engine alone did not cause everything: it was crucial, **but capital, labour, resources, agriculture and markets also mattered**.

✅ **Fact:** The trap control states it from the other direction: do not reduce the Industrial Revolution to machines alone, because the examination expects social effects.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the defensible causal placement is therefore precise. Mechanisation of spinning began in 1764 and the water frame followed in 1769, so textile mechanisation was already under way in the same era as Watt's breakthrough. Steam did not start the process; it **removed the constraint that would otherwise have stopped it**. That formulation gives steam its full weight without making it the origin, and it is the version that survives cross-examination.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Steam did not begin industrialisation; it removed the ceiling that would have ended it - and by turning power into a purchasable input it made scale a matter of investment rather than of geography.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** If steam merely removed a constraint, the emphasis placed on it is disproportionate. Water power continued to drive mills for a long time after Watt, and an account that treats steam as the hinge of the period is repeating a popular story rather than following the evidence. On this reading, steam belongs with the many improvements of the period rather than at the centre of it.

**Reply:** The continued use of water power is genuine and is exactly why the claim made here is about the *ceiling* rather than about immediate replacement. A constraint can bind long before it is universally encountered: what matters is not that every mill converted to steam quickly but that, once steam existed, the maximum feasible scale and the choice of location were no longer set by hydrology. That is a change in the structure of the possible, and structural changes show their effects through the aggregate pattern - the clustering of industry into towns and coalfields, the growth of enterprises beyond anything a stream could drive - rather than through the conversion date of any particular mill. Steam's importance is therefore correctly stated as a removed ceiling and incorrectly stated as an instant replacement.

**Residual:** This evidence base holds no data on the pace of that transition - no count of engines, no share of power capacity, no date at which steam overtook water. The claim taught here is consequently structural and qualitative, and any answer that asserts a date or a proportion for the steam-water crossover is asserting something the sources read here do not support.

### UPSC application

Steam is the single most predictable item in any question on this topic, and therefore the place where a distinctive answer is cheapest to produce: nearly every candidate will mention it, and very few will say what it removed. The two sentences that earn marks are that Watt improved rather than invented the engine, and that the engine converted power from a site-specific endowment into a purchasable input, ending the ceiling that water power imposed on mill siting and scale. For a causes question, add the placement point - steam removed a constraint on a process already under way in textiles. For a consequences question, use the loop, because it explains why the change compounded rather than plateaued. For a "how far was technology responsible" framing, the recorded caution that capital, labour, resources, agriculture and markets also mattered is the qualification that keeps the answer balanced.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Watt invented the steam engine, against an explicit recorded control.
- Making steam the cause of industrialisation, when mechanisation in textiles was already under way.
- Calling the engine "important" or "revolutionary" without naming the constraint it removed.
- Separating coal, iron and machinery into three unrelated facts and losing the self-reinforcing loop.
- Asserting a date or a proportion for the replacement of water power by steam.
- Treating the energy change as purely technical and omitting its social consequence - that mills could now cluster, which is what produced industrial towns.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Watt's steam engine takes production beyond the limits of muscle and water power.
2. ✅ Control: Watt's improvements were the decisive breakthrough for industrial use; he did not solely invent the engine.
3. ✅ Mechanism: steam removes the siting and scale limit, so output expands without waiting for water sites and unit costs keep falling.
4. ⚠️ The conversion in one line: power ceases to be a place and becomes a purchase.
5. ⚠️ Four constraints removed - site, scale, season and scarcity of suitable locations.
6. ⚠️ Clustering is the social consequence: mills near coal, labour and transport become towns.
7. ✅ Coal and iron together support machinery production; machine-building expands.
8. ⚠️ The loop: coal to steam to more coal and iron to better machines including better engines, and back to more coal.
9. ✅ Caution: the steam engine alone did not cause everything; capital, labour, resources, agriculture and markets also mattered.
10. ✅ Caution: do not reduce the Industrial Revolution to machines alone; social effects are expected.
11. ⚠️ Causal placement: steam did not initiate the process, it removed the ceiling that would have stopped it.
12. ⚠️ No engine counts, capacity shares or steam-versus-water crossover dates are available from this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The steam engine was the most important invention of the Industrial Revolution because it powered the factories." Improve the sentence so that it states a mechanism, respects the attribution control, and places steam correctly in the causal order.

**Model answer:** A corrected version runs: Watt's improvements turned an existing engine into an industrially viable source of power, and their significance lies in what they removed rather than in what they supplied - before steam, a mill had to stand where water fell, so its location was fixed by geology and its maximum size by the flow of a particular stream, whereas after steam a mill could stand wherever coal could be delivered and could be enlarged by burning more of it, which converted power from a site-specific natural endowment into a purchasable input and allowed unit costs to keep falling as scale grew. That change did not begin industrialisation, since the spinning jenny of 1764 and the water frame of 1769 had already mechanised spinning, but it removed the ceiling that would otherwise have limited the process, and it allowed mills to cluster near coal, labour and transport, which is the immediate origin of the industrial town. Its full effect appears only in combination with coal and iron, which together supported machine-building and produced a self-reinforcing loop in which better engines made more coal and iron available and more coal and iron made better engines possible.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the continued use of water power after Watt as evidence that steam has been overrated. The claim is about a removed ceiling, not about instant replacement: once steam existed, the feasible scale and location of industry were no longer decided by hydrology, and that structural change shows in the clustering of industry rather than in the conversion date of any particular mill.

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** A machine that is expensive, powered by purchased fuel and capable of running continuously cannot be scattered across cottages. It has to be housed, watched and kept running - and the people who tend it have to arrive when it starts. The next lesson is about that requirement, which turns out to be the origin of industrial discipline and of most of what workers objected to.

## Lesson 7 - The factory as a discipline: how production was reorganised, not merely relocated

Progress: 7/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the factory system replacing the putting-out system; supervision and standardisation; time-discipline and the working day; why the factory raised output per worker; what the worker lost that was not wages

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's statement that the factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system, its record of long hours and low wages in the early factory regime, and the evidence unit recording that standardised supervised production raised output per worker beyond artisan capacity
CA search: not sought - under the bounded History research rule this topic carries one current linkage only, held in Lesson 15
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four things the factory changed, and who gained each

```text
  PUTTING-OUT SYSTEM                     FACTORY SYSTEM
  ------------------------------------   ------------------------------------
  WHO OWNS THE TOOL
  the worker (spinning wheel, loom)      the employer (jenny, frame, loom,
                                         engine)
        gain: -                                gain: EMPLOYER
        the worker can withhold or             the worker cannot work without
        redirect his own tool                  entering the employer's premises

  WHO SETS THE PACE
  the worker, the season, daylight       the machine, enforced by supervision
        gain: -                                gain: EMPLOYER
        pace varies with need, health,         output per hour becomes
        harvest, custom                        predictable and maximisable

  WHO CONTROLS QUALITY AND MATERIAL
  the worker, out of sight               the employer, under observation
        gain: -                                gain: EMPLOYER
        waste and embezzlement are             standardisation becomes
        the merchant's unsolved problem        possible - which distant
                                               markets require

  WHERE WORK HAPPENS
  inside the household                   in a separate building
        gain: -                                loss: HOUSEHOLD
        work and family life are one           work and family life are
        activity                               separated in time and place

  ------------------------------------------------------------------
  THE SUMMARY THAT EARNS THE MARK:
  the factory is not the same work moved to a bigger room. It is a
  transfer of CONTROL over tools, pace, quality and location - and
  every one of the four transfers runs in the same direction.
```

*Read the "gain" lines downwards. The factory's productivity advantage and the workers' grievance are the same fact described from two ends, which is why this topic's technical and social halves cannot be separated.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

It is tempting to define a factory by its size or its machinery. Neither definition works. Large workshops existed before; so did water-powered mills. What makes the factory a new *system* is that it brings the workers to the machinery, under one roof, at one time, under supervision - and it does so because expensive, continuously running machinery makes every other arrangement impossible.

Consider the problem from the position of someone who has just bought a machine. The machine is costly and must be used as many hours as possible, or the investment is wasted. It must be maintained, because a stoppage costs money. Its output must be uniform, because a distant buyer purchasing unseen requires consistency. And the raw material fed into it must be accounted for. None of those requirements can be met if the machinery is distributed across fifty cottages worked at fifty different paces by people the owner cannot see.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the factory is therefore best defined not as a place but as a *solution*: the organisational form that expensive, continuously operating, standardising machinery requires. Define it that way in an answer and the definition immediately explains the discipline, the hours and the conflict.

### 2. The recorded claims, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** The sources record that the **factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system**.

✅ **Fact:** The evidence unit records the mechanism: **standardised, supervised production raises output per worker beyond artisan capacity**.

✅ **Fact:** The consequences table records the labour regime directly: **long hours, low wages, women and child labour**, with the analytical note that **a social question emerges**.

✅ **Fact:** The historiographical companion records the same regime from the other direction: early workers faced **long hours, slums, insecurity and child labour**.

### 3. Time-discipline: the change that is easiest to underrate

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** of all the transfers in the visual, the one that generated the most sustained conflict was the transfer of control over *pace and hours*, and it is worth stating why in a form that can be written.

Household production ran on task-time. Work expanded and contracted around the day's requirements: a harvest, an illness, a market day, a religious observance, the length of daylight. Intensity varied; nobody counted minutes. Factory production runs on clock-time, because a machine driven by a central power source starts and stops together for everybody, and a worker who arrives late has not merely lost his own hour but has left a machine idle.

| Consequence of clock-time | ⚠️ Why it follows |
|---|---|
| Fixed starting and finishing times | The power source serves all machines simultaneously |
| Fines and penalties for lateness or absence | The cost of an idle machine falls on the owner, who transfers it |
| The length of the working day becomes the central dispute | It is now a quantity fixed by the employer, not by the task |
| Rest, sociability and family time are displaced from the workplace | Work is separated in place and time from the household |
| "Long hours" becomes a specific, contestable grievance | An hour is now a countable unit that one party sets and the other endures |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last row is the bridge to Lessons 11 and 12. The working day becomes legislatable precisely because it has become countable; the early Factory Acts regulate hours because hours are the dimension in which the new regime is experienced. A grievance about task-time cannot be legislated; a grievance about a twelve-hour day can.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The factory did not merely lengthen work; it made the length of work a number set by one party and endured by another - which is why the working day, and not the wage, became the first object of industrial legislation.

### 4. Why output per worker rose: four sources, stated separately

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** candidates frequently attribute the productivity gain entirely to machinery. Machinery is one of four sources, and naming all four is a mark of understanding.

1. ⚠️ **The machine itself** performs more motions per hour than a hand can.
2. ⚠️ **Continuity** - machinery driven by a central power source runs without the interruptions of household work.
3. ⚠️ **Specialisation** - a worker who performs one operation repeatedly acquires speed that a worker performing a whole sequence cannot.
4. ✅ **Supervision and standardisation** - the recorded mechanism: standardised, supervised production raises output per worker beyond artisan capacity.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third and fourth sources cost the worker something the first two do not. Specialisation narrows skill, which reduces the worker's bargaining position: a person who can perform one operation is replaceable in a way that a person who can make a whole cloth is not. Supervision removes autonomy. So two of the four productivity sources are *achieved by reducing the worker's independence*, which is why the productivity gain and the social grievance are not separable phenomena.

### 5. What the worker lost, listed exactly

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this list matters because "exploitation" as a bare word earns nothing. Specifying the losses does.

| Lost | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|
| Ownership of the tool | No production is possible except on someone else's premises |
| Control of pace and hours | The working day becomes a quantity set by the employer |
| Breadth of skill | Replaceability rises; bargaining power falls |
| The unity of home and work | The household economy is reorganised - Lesson 10 |
| Security | Employment depends on trade conditions, not on a customary relationship |

✅ **Fact:** The advanced companion records the resulting position exactly: **landless, toolless workers depended on employers**, so that **economic insecurity becomes structural**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word "structural" carries the argument. The insecurity was not the result of a harsh employer or a bad year; it was a property of the arrangement. That is why the response, when it came, took institutional forms - unions, statutes, a demand for the vote - rather than appeals to individual benevolence.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The account romanticises the putting-out system. Cottage work was not autonomous in any meaningful sense: households were dependent on a merchant who set piece rates, they worked extremely long hours when rates were low, child labour was normal in the cottage, and the household's "control of pace" was largely the freedom to work harder for the same income. If so, the factory changed the setting of exploitation rather than introducing it.

**Reply:** The objection is substantially correct and improves the argument rather than defeating it. The claim being made is not that cottage work was pleasant or free but that the *form* of dependence changed, and that the change had consequences. Under putting-out, the worker was dependent on a merchant for materials and orders while retaining the tool, the premises and the disposal of his own time; under the factory system he was dependent for all four. Both are forms of subordination; only the second makes attendance, pace and hours enforceable by another party. That distinction is what makes hours legislatable and what makes collective action inside a single workplace possible. The objection therefore corrects an idealisation without touching the mechanism, and the strongest version of the argument concedes it explicitly: the factory intensified and concentrated a dependence that already existed, and by concentrating it made it visible, measurable and eventually contestable.

**Residual:** This evidence base records "long hours" without recording their length, and records "low wages" without recording any rate or comparison. Nor does it record how quickly the factory system displaced putting-out in any trade. The teaching here is therefore qualitative throughout, and a candidate who writes a specific figure for the working day or a wage comparison is importing material this session cannot support.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the analytical centre of every question about social consequences and every question about what made the factory system a decisive break - a framing that appears directly in the topic's probable-question bank. Its highest-value move is the definition: the factory as the organisational form that expensive, continuously running, standardising machinery requires, rather than as a large building with machines in it. Follow that with the four transfers of control and the four sources of productivity gain, note that two of those sources are achieved by reducing the worker's independence, and the answer has explained both the economic advantage and the social grievance from a single mechanism. For a "how did it affect society" framing, the list of specific losses in section 5 is more valuable than any adjective.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Defining the factory by size or by the presence of machines, which yields no explanation of why it arose.
- Attributing the whole productivity gain to machinery and omitting continuity, specialisation and supervision.
- Using "exploitation" as a conclusion instead of specifying which controls transferred and what was lost.
- Romanticising the putting-out system as free or comfortable, which the strongest objection rightly attacks.
- Supplying a number for the working day or a wage rate, neither of which this evidence base holds.
- Treating the factory's discipline as harshness rather than as a requirement generated by capital tied up in machinery.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system.
2. ✅ Mechanism: standardised, supervised production raises output per worker beyond artisan capacity.
3. ✅ The early regime: long hours, low wages, women's and children's labour; a social question emerges.
4. ✅ The companion's version: long hours, slums, insecurity and child labour.
5. ⚠️ Definition to use: the factory is the organisational form required by expensive, continuously running, standardising machinery.
6. ⚠️ Four transfers of control - tool, pace, quality and material, location - all running in the same direction.
7. ⚠️ Task-time gives way to clock-time; fixed hours, fines and the length of the working day as the central dispute.
8. ⚠️ Four sources of higher output per worker - the machine, continuity, specialisation, supervision - two of which cost the worker independence.
9. ⚠️ Specific losses: tool ownership, control of hours, breadth of skill, unity of home and work, security.
10. ✅ Landless, toolless workers depended on employers, so economic insecurity becomes structural.
11. ⚠️ Hours become legislatable because they have become countable - the bridge to the Factory Acts.
12. ⚠️ No figures for hours, wages or the pace of displacement are available from this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the factory system, rather than machinery as such, is the decisive break from earlier production - and state the strongest objection to your own answer together with your reply.

**Model answer:** Machinery by itself changes how fast a task is done; the factory system changes who controls the task, and it is the change of control that reorganised society. The sources record that the factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system and that standardised supervised production raised output per worker beyond artisan capacity, and both claims describe control rather than speed: the employer now owns the tool, so no work can be done except on his premises; the machine and its supervisor set the pace, so the working day becomes a quantity fixed by one party; quality and materials are observed, so standardisation becomes possible; and work is separated from the household in place and time. Two of the four sources of the productivity gain - specialisation, which narrows skill and raises replaceability, and supervision, which removes autonomy - are obtained precisely by reducing the worker's independence, which is why the economic advantage and the social grievance are one phenomenon seen from two ends. The strongest objection is that this romanticises cottage work, which was also dependent, also long-houred and also worked by children, so that the factory merely relocated exploitation. The reply is that the objection corrects an idealisation without touching the mechanism: under putting-out the worker depended on a merchant for materials and orders but retained tool, premises and the disposal of his own time, whereas the factory made all four dependent, and only that concentration made hours enforceable, measurable, legislatable and collectively contestable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating factory discipline as the personal harshness of employers. The discipline follows from capital tied up in machinery that loses money when idle, which is why it appears wherever such machinery appears and why it was contested through hours legislation rather than through appeals to individual conduct.

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** Output can now be produced in quantities that no local market can absorb, at a cost advantage that exists at the factory gate. Whether that advantage still exists at the point of sale depends on what it costs to move goods - which is the subject of the next lesson, and of the one examination question this topic owns outright.

## Lesson 8 - Rails, canals and roads: why the same technology produced opposite social results

Progress: 8/17 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: canals, macadam roads and steam boats; the first passenger railway, 1830; delivered cost and the survival of a cost advantage; the five country types and the differentiated social effects; the common effects of rail everywhere

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's transport row on railways, canals, macadam roads and steam boats, its dated marker for the first passenger railway, and the differentiated evidence bank that separates railway effects by country type across early industrialiser, continental catch-up, settler frontier, late state-assisted and colonial settings, together with its list of common social effects

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one technology, five economies, five outcomes
