Step five: because the insecurity was structural rather than personal, no individual conduct could remedy it, and the demand therefore became institutional - statutory protection in 1802, lawful combination in 1824, and the franchise in the Chartism of the 1830s and 1840s. The household enters at step three and the town at step four, and neither is an addendum: they are the two sites where the technical changes became social ones.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the social consequences as unfortunate side-effects of a technical process. Each step follows from the previous one, which is why an answer that derives them scores higher than one that lists them, and why the productivity gain and the social grievance cannot be separated.

### Cumulative check C2 - why laissez-faire weakened without being defeated

**Question:** Explain why an order committed to minimum interference generated its own regulation, and use the two recorded cautions to keep the account from becoming a story of progress.

**Model answer:** The industrial order produced three problems for which non-interference had no remedy. Hours became a fixed countable quantity set by the employer, which made them both a grievance and a legislatable object. The mill town produced a sanitary and housing crisis that fell outside every established duty, since an employer's obligation ended at the gate and parish structures could not drain a district that had not existed a decade earlier. And the employment of children raised a harm that could not be described as free contract, because a child cannot be said to have consented - which is precisely why child labour could be regulated in 1802 without abandoning the principle that free adults may contract as they wish. Workers then forced legal recognition of combination in 1824, and carried the demand to the constitution in the 1830s and 1840s, because protection defined by others is revocable and organisation governed by others is containable. The two cautions prevent this from reading as steady progress: the early Acts were weakly enforced, which is exactly what one expects of a concession designed to address visible harm without disturbing the governing doctrine; and Chartism did not achieve its immediate aims, so the sequence ends in an unmet demand rather than a settlement. Laissez-faire was therefore eroded from inside the industrial order by harms that order itself produced, not defeated from outside by its ideological opponents.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the reforms as evidence of state benevolence or of working-class strength. A legislature elected on a narrow property franchise conceded the minimum that would address an intolerable and highly visible situation, which is why enforcement was weak and why the demand for political rights followed rather than preceded the concessions.

## Block D - Diffusion, the latecomer and the world, Lessons 13 to 15

### Cumulative check D1 - unevenness as evidence

**Question:** Show how the uneven spread of industrialisation functions as positive evidence for the causal account of Lessons 3 and 4, and name the axis on which the cases should be organised.

**Model answer:** The spread was uneven by the sources' explicit statement: it began in England, reached France and Belgium after 1815, came to Germany later but powerfully, transformed the United States especially after 1870, and reached Russia and Japan later still on state-assisted paths. That unevenness is not merely a caution against generalisation; it is evidence. If coal or capital or overseas markets or secure contracts had been sufficient, industrialisation would have begun simultaneously wherever each existed, and it did not - which supports the conjunction claim directly and defeats every single-cause account in turn. It also shows that industrialisation was not a stage through which societies pass automatically, since several economies held several conditions for decades without the process beginning. The axis on which the cases should be organised is therefore not early against late but who initiated and under what compulsion: private enterprise responding to market advantage in Britain and the United States, national integration doing decisive work in Germany, and state security driving the programme in Japan and Russia. That axis explains why later industrialisations were structurally different rather than merely later, since a latecomer can import proven technology and copy institutions but must compete against an incumbent that is already cheaper, and the American case supplies the dated evidence for the resulting change in hierarchy - a vast and successful industrial revolution mainly in the last quarter of the nineteenth century, with coal, iron and steel output surpassing Britain and Germany by 1914.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the 1914 American overtaking as a general claim of economic superiority. The source measures it on coal, iron and steel output only, and extending it to technology, finance or living standards attributes to the evidence a scope it does not have.

### Cumulative check D2 - the outward pressure and its two boundaries

**Question:** State the relationship between industrialisation and empire in the form that survives cross-examination, and identify the two boundaries that govern how far this topic may take the argument.

**Model answer:** The defensible formulation is that industrial capitalism did not mechanically create empire but greatly intensified the pressure for it, converting colonies from trading posts into structurally required sources of raw material and captive demand - which is why imperial competition intensified precisely as industrial rivalry did. That formulation is stronger than a causal claim rather than weaker, because empires existed long before industry, so industry cannot have invented the impulse, while colonies were reorganised for extraction and captive demand in ways pre-industrial trading empires had not required, so industry plainly changed what empire was for. It also resists the opposite error, since the sources record that imperialism cannot be explained only by racism or only by nationalism and that the economic demands generated by industrial capitalism are central. The first boundary is that the ranked drivers of New Imperialism, the partition of Africa and the arrangements that regulated it belong to the imperialism topic; this topic supplies the industrial pressure and routes the partition. The second is that the "how far" verdict on Indian handicraft decline belongs to the Modern Indian History owner, which holds tariff asymmetry, lost patronage, revenue policy, transport penetration and craft-wise and regional variation; this topic supplies the five-step English mechanism and the hand-off sentence, and does not re-derive Indian causes from English evidence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the intensification formula as a compromise between two positions. It is a distinct and more precise claim: that industry changed the function of empire rather than its existence, and the evidence for it is the difference between a trading post and a colony reorganised as a supplier and a market.

## Block E - Judgement and synthesis, Lessons 16 to 17

### Cumulative check E1 - four debates, four distinctions

**Question:** Name the four debates on this topic and the distinction that settles each, and say why the resolutions take the form of distinctions rather than verdicts.

**Model answer:** The first debate is whether the process deserves the name revolution, and it is settled by separating impact from pace: the term names irreversibility of structure rather than speed of diffusion, so a process can be gradual in spread and revolutionary in consequence. The second is whether it was a blessing, and it is settled by separating short-run misery from long-run productivity gain and then stating the distribution - gains general and delayed, costs concentrated and immediate. The third is what caused it, and it is settled by the sufficiency test: every candidate single cause existed somewhere that did not industrialise first, so the explanation must be a conjunction, ranked by removal rather than by assertion. The fourth is the relation between market and state, and it is settled by showing why labour laws and the modern state grew, which converts a values dispute into an evidential one about what an order reveals when it generates problems its own doctrine cannot address. The resolutions take the form of distinctions because in each case both sides are asserting something true about a different object - magnitude against pace, the economy against particular people, a necessary condition against a sufficient one, the sources of growth against the sources of regulation - so declaring a winner would require denying evidence that the sources themselves record, while drawing the distinction preserves both and still yields a position.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a balanced historiography paragraph declines to decide. Balance comes from conceding the strongest opposing case and naming what remains open; a paragraph with no position reads as absent judgement rather than as fairness.

### Cumulative check E2 - choosing the architecture under pressure

**Question:** A 15-mark question reads: "Examine the social consequences of the Industrial Revolution." State the directive row, the architecture, the four evidence areas you would use, and the qualification that must appear.

**Model answer:** The directive row is the social-consequences row, whose real demand is class, gender, childhood, city, health and protest, and whose recorded failure mode is the empty formulation that it brought progress and problems. At 15 marks the architecture is a thesis, four or five named conditions or mechanisms compressed into one movement, the consequences across class, gender and city, the state and labour response, a qualification on unevenness and a verdict. The thesis is that industrialisation raised productive capacity faster than it raised welfare, and that the gap between the two produced the modern social question. The four evidence areas are: class, using the separation of ownership from working, the industrial bourgeoisie and the wage proletariat, and the structural insecurity of landless and toolless workers; gender and childhood, using the centrality of women's and children's labour to the early factory regime, the restructuring of the household economy and the caution that domestic and outwork labour continued alongside factory employment; the city, using the shift of economic life from village to city, slum conditions in the early phases and the emergence of public health, housing and sanitation as state problems; and protest and law, using the Factory Act of 1802, the repeal of the anti-union laws in 1824 and Chartism in the 1830s and 1840s, with the weak early enforcement and Chartism's immediate failure attached. The qualification that must appear is unevenness - urbanisation was concentrated in specific industrial regions, conditions varied by trade and improved unevenably over the century, and displacement was sequential rather than simultaneous, since cheap machine yarn expanded hand weaving before the power loom removed it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Spending the answer on adjectives. "Exploitation", "misery" and "squalor" are conclusions; the marks lie in specifying which controls transferred, which groups were affected, what the household lost and which institutional responses followed.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These six questions are original to this session and are not verified PYQs. Attempt each under timed conditions before reading the model answer. Each model sits at or below its stated word ceiling, and each carries a scoring note identifying the single element that most often separates a good script from an average one on that demand.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain why the factory system, rather than mechanisation alone, marked the decisive break from earlier forms of production. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

Mechanisation changes how fast a task is done; the factory system changes who controls it, and control is what reorganised society. The factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system, under which a merchant supplied materials while the household kept its tools, premises and pace. Expensive, continuously running machinery made that arrangement impossible: output had to be supervised, standardised and accounted for. Four controls therefore transferred to the employer - tool, pace, quality and place - and standardised supervised production raised output per worker beyond artisan capacity. Two of the four sources of that gain, specialisation and supervision, were obtained by reducing the worker's independence, which is why the productivity advantage and the grievance were one phenomenon. The decisive consequence was structural: landless, toolless workers now depended on employers, and the working day became a quantity fixed by one party and endured by another.

*Scoring note: the mark separator here is the word "control". A script that lists machines and buildings describes the change; a script that names the four transfers explains it, and only the explanation connects to the social consequences the examiner expects next.*

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> "The Industrial Revolution began in England because of a conjunction, not a cause." Examine. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

The claim is defensible because every proposed single cause existed elsewhere without producing industrialisation. England had abundant coal and iron, but so did regions that did not industrialise first: resources alone explain nothing. Capital came from expanding trade, colonies and plundered wealth, yet colonial profit is recorded as one source among several. Agricultural improvement through crop rotation, manuring and new implements fed a non-farming population but was regionally uneven. Enclosure supplied workers with no alternative subsistence, who therefore accepted factory time-discipline, though it was a long contested process. Overseas and domestic markets justified production far beyond local need, and a post-seventeenth-century state lowered expropriation risk and enforced contracts, though its stability was comparative only. Each is necessary and none sufficient, so the explanation must be their simultaneous presence. The permitted ranking is that removing one element slows the sequence; removing secure markets and cheap coal together prevents it.

*Scoring note: the separator is the sufficiency test applied explicitly to at least three conditions. Conceding insufficiency is what makes the conjunction claim an argument rather than a hedge, and scripts that assert conjunction without testing it read as evasion.*

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Examine the view that the social costs of the Industrial Revolution fell on groups other than those who received its benefits. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The view is defensible provided the asymmetry is specified rather than asserted: industrialisation raised productive capacity faster than welfare, and the gap produced the modern social question.

The costs were concentrated and immediate. The early factory regime is recorded as long hours, low wages, and women's and children's labour, with slum conditions in the early phases and structural insecurity for landless, toolless workers dependent on employers. Displacement was sequential: cheap machine yarn from the jenny of 1764 and water frame of 1769 first expanded hand weaving, which the power loom of 1785 undercut, so a recently grown trade collapsed. Women's and children's labour was central, not incidental, because the machine supplied force and the task required attendance more than strength; the household was restructured as care and work became incompatible in time and place.

The benefits were general and delayed. Output rose, goods became cheaper and long-run prosperity expanded, but those gains accrued over decades to populations far wider than the districts that bore the cost, and urbanisation was concentrated in specific regions.

The asymmetry explains the politics: because those who paid saw benefits accruing elsewhere within their own lifetimes, the demand was for a share rather than the destruction of industry - statutory protection in 1802, lawful combination in 1824, the franchise demanded in the Chartism of the 1830s and 1840s.

The qualified verdict is that costs were unequally distributed and immediate while gains were general and deferred, though no aggregate measure of either is available.

*Scoring note: the separator is the final move, which converts a distributional observation into a historical explanation of why the response took legal and constitutional rather than anti-industrial forms. Scripts that stop at "the poor suffered while the rich gained" have described the asymmetry without using it.*

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> "Railways magnified the economic order they entered." Discuss with reference to different types of economy. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The statement is correct and is the only formulation explaining why one technology produced opposite social results. A railway lowers the cost of moving goods between two chosen points; what follows depends on who chose them and on what the economy was already organised to do.

In the early industrialiser, railways completed a transport revolution begun by canals and macadam roads, integrated a national market, cheapened coal and raw-material movement, and pulled forward iron, coal and engineering demand. Chronology matters: the first passenger railway is dated 1830, decades after the first textile machinery, so railways followed mechanisation rather than causing it.

In continental catch-up economies, construction was often state-promoted and served national economic integration; in the German case a market was unified before a state was.

On the settler frontier, growth accelerated especially after 1870 as railways opened interior land to settlement and commercial agriculture, binding a continental market - while the same expansion dispossessed indigenous populations.

In late, state-assisted paths, railways were instruments of deliberate state modernisation and strategic reach rather than spontaneous market outcomes, and compressed modernisation carried heavier social strain.

In colonial economies, lines typically ran from producing districts to ports and inland for administration and troops, deepening export dependence instead of building a diversified home market.

Common effects followed everywhere: urbanisation and commuting, standardised national time, cheaper migration, new occupational groups, and a capacity both to relieve famine and to worsen it. Integration or extraction was decided by ownership and prior organisation, not by technology.

*Scoring note: the separator is the famine sentence, which proves the thesis on a single commodity in a single season and shows that the differentiation is one mechanism rather than five memorised country boxes.*

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Critically examine the proposition that the Industrial Revolution transformed the relationship between the state, the employer and the worker. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The proposition holds, provided the transformation is shown to be generated from inside the industrial order rather than imposed upon it.

Previously the state's economic functions were fiscal, military and legal. The industrial order then produced three problems that minimum interference could not address. Hours became a countable quantity fixed by the employer, and therefore a legislatable object. The mill town, built workplace-first, produced a sanitary crisis belonging to no existing institution, so public health, housing and sanitation became state problems for the first time. Child labour raised a harm that could not be described as free contract.

The response climbed a ladder. The First Factory Act of 1802 began regulating labour and hours, and laissez-faire weakened. The repeal of the anti-union laws in 1824 made trade-union growth possible; workers forced legal recognition over time. Chartism in the 1830s and 1840s converted the social question into a constitutional one.

Two cautions prevent a progress narrative: early Acts were weakly enforced, and Chartism did not achieve its immediate aims. A legislature elected on a narrow property franchise conceded the minimum that would restore order, which is why enforcement was thin and political rights were demanded afterwards.

The transformation was plural, not uniform. Industrialisation did not mean free trade everywhere, since many late industrialisers used protection; and where the state initiated industrialisation, as in the latecomer route from 1868, workers' attempts to organise politically were suppressed rather than legalised.

The verdict: the relationship was permanently altered, unevenly and under pressure.

*Scoring note: the separator is the comparative final movement. A script confined to Britain can describe the change; one that contrasts a market-led route answering grievance with law and a state-led route answering it with suppression has shown that the outcome depended on who initiated industrialisation.*

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> "Industrial capitalism raised the stakes of empire rather than inventing the impulse." Critically examine, with attention to the limits of the economic explanation. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The formulation is more precise than either alternative and should be defended against both.

The economic mechanism is real and specific. Rising industrial output generated a need for raw materials, markets and investment outlets; that need produced competition among industrial powers, and competition produced colonial domination and imperial rivalry. Industrialisation intensified the search for markets and raw materials, and industrial countries subordinated the economies of weaker regions to their own interests. The dependence was structural from the outset: the leading sector's raw fibre was imported, and the machine that cleared its bottleneck in 1793 was invented across an ocean, in the economy supplying the material.

Against causation, empires long predate industry, so industry cannot have invented the impulse. What changed was empire's function: colonies became required suppliers and captive markets rather than trading posts.

Against the opposite error, imperialism cannot be explained only by racism or only by nationalism; the economic demands generated by industrial capitalism are central, and an account resting solely on prestige or ideology cannot explain the reorganisation that followed conquest.

Three limits must be conceded. Not every colony was profitable. The ranked drivers of late-nineteenth-century partition, including strategic and prestige motives, belong to the topic owning them. And the verdict on any colonial economy - notably the extent of Indian handicraft decline - depends on local tariff, patronage and demand conditions, since cheaper competing supply is a pressure, not an automatic outcome.

Industrial capitalism therefore changed what empire was for, without being its sole cause.

*Scoring note: the separator is the concession of three limits without retreating from the claim. Scripts that either assert economic causation or reduce empire to ideology both fail; the mark lies in showing that a changed function is a stronger and more defensible claim than a claimed origin.*

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it costs marks | The correction | Taught in |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Opening with a list of inventions and inventors | Answers "what was invented" when the question asked "what changed" | Open with the structural definition - an agrarian and handicraft economy converted into an industrial one | Lesson 1 |
| 2 | Using "factory system" and "industrialisation" as synonyms | Loses the supervision argument that explains why factories existed | The factory is the organisational form required by expensive, continuously running, standardising machinery | Lessons 1, 7 |
| 3 | Dating the Industrial Revolution to a single year | Breaches an explicit recorded control | "From about the middle of the eighteenth century", or "c. 1760s-1840s" for a span | Lesson 2 |
| 4 | Crediting Watt with inventing the steam engine | A recorded factual error | Watt's improvements were the decisive breakthrough for industrial use | Lessons 2, 6 |
| 5 | Treating the cotton gin as British | A recorded factual error, and it loses the transatlantic dependence point | Eli Whitney, 1793, in the United States | Lessons 2, 5 |
| 6 | Supplying British output, wage or population figures | No such figure is held in this evidence base | Replace the number with a dated marker and a named mechanism | Lesson 2 |
| 7 | Naming a single decisive cause of British priority | Every candidate cause existed elsewhere without the same result | A conjunction, ranked by the removal test | Lessons 3, 4, 16 |
| 8 | Omitting agriculture from the conditions | Agriculture is logically prior to the urban workforce every other condition presupposes | Crop rotation, manuring and new implements fed a non-farming population | Lesson 3 |
| 9 | Treating enclosure only as labour supply | Misses the mechanism that made factory terms acceptable | It supplied workers with no alternative subsistence who therefore accepted time-discipline | Lesson 3 |
| 10 | Naming conditions as possessions | "England had coal" explains nothing | Name the constraint each condition removed | Lesson 4 |
| 11 | Making empire the single cause of British industrialisation | Breaches a recorded caution | Colonial profit is one source among several | Lessons 4, 16 |
| 12 | Reducing demand to exports | Breaches a recorded caution | Domestic demand also mattered | Lesson 4 |
| 13 | Treating "stability" as tranquillity or good government | Overstates what the sources support | Security of commercial expectations; stability is comparative | Lesson 4 |
| 14 | Listing the four textile machines without the sequence | Keeps the trivia and loses the mechanism | Each machine created the constraint the next solved | Lesson 5 |
| 15 | Claiming mechanisation destroyed handwork immediately | Ignores the recorded sequence | Cheap machine yarn expanded hand weaving before the power loom removed it | Lesson 5 |
| 16 | Making steam the cause of industrialisation | Contradicted by the dates | Steam removed the ceiling on a process already under way | Lesson 6 |
| 17 | Separating coal, iron and machinery | Loses the self-reinforcing loop | Coal, steam, iron and machine-building compound one another | Lesson 6 |
| 18 | Using "exploitation" as a conclusion | An adjective in place of an analysis | Specify the four transfers of control and the five specific losses | Lesson 7 |
| 19 | Romanticising the putting-out system | The objection is correct about cottage dependence | Concede it; the change was in the form of dependence, which made hours legislatable | Lesson 7 |
| 20 | Writing one undifferentiated paragraph on railways | The recorded failure mode on the owned question | Differentiate by country type, then state the magnification rule | Lesson 8 |
| 21 | Presenting railways as a cause of British industrialisation | Contradicted by the dates and by a recorded caution | Railways followed the first mechanisation wave | Lessons 4, 8 |
| 22 | Omitting the colonial case from a railways answer | The "different countries" phrase is aimed there | Lines to ports deepened export dependence | Lesson 8 |
| 23 | Omitting indigenous dispossession from the settler-frontier case | The stem asked for social effects | Say it explicitly where the question asks for social effects | Lesson 8 |
| 24 | Defining class by income | Makes the later politics inexplicable | Class here is a relation to the means of production | Lesson 9 |
| 25 | Writing that the Industrial Revolution "created inequality" | Inequality preceded it | What changed was the axis along which the main division ran | Lesson 9 |
| 26 | Treating urbanisation as uniform | Breaches a recorded caution | It was concentrated in specific industrial regions | Lesson 9 |
| 27 | Describing slums without the analytical step | Loses the argument about state responsibility | The crisis was unassignable and therefore became a state problem | Lesson 9 |
| 28 | Mentioning women and children only as victims | Explains nothing about why the labour was wanted | Derive usability from force, task narrowness, attendance and supplementary pay | Lesson 10 |
| 29 | Writing a home-to-factory substitution narrative for women | Breaches an explicit recorded caution | Factory work coexisted with continued domestic and outwork labour | Lesson 10 |
| 30 | Writing "mixed blessing" without specifying the mixture | Converts the source's own position into a hedge | Gains general and delayed; costs concentrated and immediate | Lesson 11 |
| 31 | Asserting wage, price, height or mortality evidence | No such data is held here | Teach the structure of the debate and refuse the number | Lessons 11, 16 |
| 32 | Listing 1802, 1824 and Chartism without the causal argument | The recorded failure mode on the state question | Explain why each concession proved insufficient | Lesson 12 |
| 33 | Omitting the weak-enforcement and Chartism-failure cautions | Produces a progress narrative | Attach both cautions every time | Lesson 12 |
| 34 | Writing that industrialisation meant free trade everywhere | Breaches a recorded trap | Many late industrialisers used protection | Lessons 12, 16 |
| 35 | Claiming even European diffusion | Breaches a recorded trap | It began in England and spread unevenly | Lesson 13 |
| 36 | Using the 1914 American overtaking as general superiority | Extends the source beyond its scope | It measures coal, iron and steel output only | Lesson 13 |
| 37 | Writing two parallel narratives in a comparison question | Leaves the comparison to the examiner | Run one organising question through both cases, axis by axis | Lesson 14 |
| 38 | Naming Japanese business combines as sourced fact | Breaches an explicit recorded control | "Concentrated business groups closely tied to government", with the standard term marked as inference | Lesson 14 |
| 39 | Asserting Meiji model-enterprise sales or land-tax reform | Neither is supported by this evidence base | Railways, roads, and modern cotton and silk industry from 1868 | Lesson 14 |
| 40 | Ending the Japanese account at 1905 or 1918 | Omits what the same source uses to explain the sequel | Include the mid-1921 collapse and the 1929 shock | Lesson 14 |
| 41 | Writing "industry caused empire" | Breaches a recorded caution | Intensification, not automatic causation | Lesson 15 |
| 42 | Explaining imperialism only by racism or nationalism | Breaches the same caution from the other side | The economic demands of industrial capitalism are central | Lesson 15 |
| 43 | Answering the England-and-India question from world history alone | The recorded non-negotiable boundary failure | Supply the mechanism, use the hand-off, route the verdict | Lesson 15 |
| 44 | Asserting that the Industrial Revolution destroyed Indian handicrafts | Fails a "how far" directive | A pressure, not an automatic outcome; decline was uneven by craft, region and segment | Lesson 15 |
| 45 | Naming historians for the four debates | This evidence base records positions without attributions | State the positions and resolve them by distinction | Lesson 16 |
| 46 | Resolving every debate in a historiography paragraph | Reads as over-confidence | Name what remains open | Lesson 16 |
| 47 | Spending 10-mark words on historiography | The mark-scaled structure reserves it for 20 marks | At 10 marks write mechanism | Lesson 17 |
| 48 | Closing without a verdict, or with one that concedes nothing | An assertion rather than a judgement | Concede something specific inside the closing sentence | Lesson 17 |

## Corrective drills

Attempt each in two or three sentences, from memory, before checking the lesson named in the error map.

### Drill 1 - the structural opening

Rewrite "The Industrial Revolution was a period of great inventions that changed Britain" so that it defines the change structurally and names the system that was replaced.

### Drill 2 - the putting-out baseline

Describe the putting-out system in one sentence, then state in one further sentence why it could not accommodate expensive machinery.

### Drill 3 - the irreversibility test

State the test by which "revolution" survives the objection that the process was gradual, and name the four things that changed without return.

### Drill 4 - the dating conventions

Give the two permitted start conventions, the phrasing true on both, and the four dating or attribution errors the controls forbid.

### Drill 5 - the lag argument

Use 1764, 1802, 1824 and the 1830s-40s to argue in three sentences that the state's response was reactive.

### Drill 6 - necessary and sufficient

Take any two conditions of British priority and apply the three tests - necessary, sufficient, mechanism - to each.

### Drill 7 - enclosure's second function

State the difference between enclosure making an industrial workforce *available* and making it *willing*.

### Drill 8 - removed constraints

Convert "England had coal", "England had capital" and "England had markets" into three removed-constraint sentences.

### Drill 9 - the circularity break

Explain in two sentences why the account of British industrialisation is not viciously circular, naming where the first round of capital and demand came from.

### Drill 10 - the bottleneck chase

Reconstruct the 1764-1793 sequence and say at which point the constraint moved backwards rather than forwards.

### Drill 11 - the delayed displacement

Explain why handloom weaving expanded before it collapsed, and what that implies for writing about the social effects of mechanisation.

### Drill 12 - the gin's location

State in one sentence what the cotton gin's invention in the United States shows about the British cotton industry.

### Drill 13 - power as a purchase

Explain what Watt's improvements removed, using the words "site", "scale" and "season".

### Drill 14 - the coal-iron-machine loop

Draw the loop in four steps and mark which two links are recorded facts and which two are inferences joining them.

### Drill 15 - the four transfers

Name the four controls that transferred to the employer, and state which party gained each.

### Drill 16 - clock-time

Explain why the length of the working day, rather than the wage, became the first object of industrial legislation.

### Drill 17 - four sources of productivity

Name the four sources of higher output per worker and identify the two that were obtained by reducing the worker's independence.

### Drill 18 - the magnification rule

State the railway thesis in one sentence and then generate two of the five country cases from it.

### Drill 19 - famine both ways

Explain how a single railway line can both relieve and worsen a famine, and what that proves about the technology.

### Drill 20 - class as a relation

Define the industrial bourgeoisie and the wage proletariat without using the words "rich" or "poor".

### Drill 21 - the unassignable problem

Explain why the sanitary crisis of the mill town became a state problem, using the word "sequence".

### Drill 22 - why this labour was usable

Give the four properties of factory work that made women's and children's labour attractive to it.

### Drill 23 - the two-trajectory caution

State what the caution forbids, and rewrite "women moved from the home to the factory" correctly.

### Drill 24 - the shaped verdict

Convert "mixed blessing" into a graded verdict in one sentence containing the words "general", "delayed", "concentrated" and "immediate".

### Drill 25 - the four reasons an aggregate fails

List the four reasons why an average living-standards figure would describe nobody.

### Drill 26 - the ladder

Give the four rungs from condition to constitution and one reason why each rung proved insufficient.

### Drill 27 - erosion from inside

Explain in two sentences why laissez-faire weakened without being defeated by its ideological opponents.

### Drill 28 - the diffusion axis

Replace "early versus late" with the correct organising axis and assign three countries to it.

### Drill 29 - the American evidence, bounded

State what the 1914 overtaking measures and what it does not, and give the second point the American case supplies.

### Drill 30 - the initiating contrast

State the Britain-Japan organising contrast in one sentence, then give three axes that develop it.

### Drill 31 - the Japanese controls

List the three controls governing Japanese material and the specific claims each forbids.

### Drill 32 - intensification

Explain why "industrial capitalism raised the stakes of empire" is a stronger claim than "industry caused empire".

### Drill 33 - the five-step mechanism

Reproduce the English mechanism in five steps with a date or named item attached to each.

### Drill 34 - the hand-off

Write the hand-off sentence from memory and state which owner holds the material it hands over.

### Drill 35 - the four distinctions

Name the four debates and the distinction that settles each, in four lines.

### Drill 36 - reading the directive

For three stems of your choice from the directive map, name the demand, the bank and the closing verdict scaffold.

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

| Date | Marker | Function in the argument |
|---|---|---|
| c. 1750 | The process begins in England | The narrative account's start convention |
| c. 1760s-1840s | The period span | The comparative chronology's convention, for placing the topic |
| 1764 | Spinning jenny - Hargreaves | Mechanisation begins; the constraint moves to weaving |
| 1769 | Water frame - Arkwright; the era of Watt's breakthrough | Mechanisation deepens; power begins to be purchasable |
| 1785 | Power loom - Cartwright | Weaving catches up; the constraint moves back to raw fibre |
| 1793 | Cotton gin - Eli Whitney, United States | The fibre bottleneck clears; intercontinental dependence is visible |
| 1802 | First Factory Act | The state enters the workplace; laissez-faire weakens |
| after 1815 | Industrial growth in France, Belgium, Switzerland | Continental catch-up begins |
| 1824 | Anti-union laws repealed | Collective action becomes lawful |
| 1830 | First railway for passengers | National market integration deepens |
| 1830s-40s | Chartism | The social question becomes constitutional |
| 1853 | Perry arrives at Yokohama | The latecomer sequence begins with an external shock |
| 1868 | Meiji restoration | Centralisation, then state-directed industrialisation |
| after 1870 | American acceleration | A later, differently based industrial surge |
| 1889 / 1890 | Japanese constitution accepted; first Diet meets | Institutions built as part of an industrial programme |
| 1894-95 / 1904-5 | Japan's wars with China and Russia | Latecomer industry changes the international balance |
| 1914 | United States surpasses Britain and Germany in coal, iron and steel | The hierarchy has changed |
| 1914-18 | Japanese cotton-cloth exports almost treble; merchant fleet doubles in tonnage | A latecomer grows when incumbents are occupied |
| mid-1921 | Japan's trade boom ends | Export dependence exposed |
| 1929-32 | World crisis; Japanese raw silk below one-fifth of its 1923 price | The compressed model breaks |

## The causal engine

```text
  [A] CONDITIONS, PRESENT TOGETHER IN ONE ECONOMY
      agriculture . enclosure . coal . iron . capital . markets
      institutions . transport
                |  each necessary, none sufficient
                v
  [B] LEADING SECTOR CHOSEN BY THE MARKET, NOT BY DESIGN
      cotton textiles: universal demand + decomposable work
      + an existing merchant organisation
                |
                v
  [C] THE BOTTLENECK CHASE, 1764 -> 1793
      jenny -> water frame -> power loom -> gin
                |
                v
  [D] STEAM REMOVES THE CEILING
      power becomes a purchase; unit costs keep falling;
      coal + iron + machinery close a self-reinforcing loop
                |
                v
  [E] THE FACTORY SOLVES THE SUPERVISION PROBLEM
      four controls transfer: tool, pace, quality, place
                |
      +---------+-------------------------------+
      |                                         |
      v                                         v
  [F] ECONOMIC OUTPUT                      [G] SOCIAL STRUCTURE
      output per worker rises;                  urbanisation; industrial
      transport carries the advantage           bourgeoisie and wage
      to distant markets                        proletariat; women's and
      |                                         children's labour;
      |                                         restructured household;
      |                                         structural insecurity
      |                                              |
      |                                              v
      |                                     [H] THE SOCIAL QUESTION
      |                                         1802 -> 1824 -> Chartism;
      |                                         laissez-faire eroded from
      |                                         inside
      v
  [I] OUTWARD PRESSURE
      raw materials + markets + investment outlets
      |
      +----------------+----------------+
      |                |                |
      v                v                v
  [J] DIFFUSION   [K] EMPIRE        [L] ENVIRONMENT
      uneven;         intensified,      concentrated smoke,
      initiating      not invented      waterway pollution,
      agent varies    |                 occupational disease
      |               |                 |
      +-------+-------+-----------------+
              |
              v
  [M] A DIVIDED WORLD ECONOMY
      uneven industrialisation -> international division of labour
              |
              v
  [N] THE JUDGEMENT
      revolution in consequence, gradual in pace;
      gains general and delayed, costs concentrated and immediate
```

## The conditions, as a single comparison table

| Condition | Constraint removed | Recorded limitation | Sufficient alone? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Agricultural surplus | The subsistence limit on town growth | Regionally uneven | No |
| Enclosure and released labour | The alternative to accepting factory discipline | A long, contested process | No |
| Coal | The water-power ceiling on siting and scale | Resources alone explain nothing | No |
| Iron | The material limit on machine-building | Depends on coal for smelting and power | No |
| Capital | The impossibility of investment before returns | Colonial profit is one source among several | No |
| Markets | The pointlessness of producing beyond local need | Domestic demand also mattered | No |
| Institutions | Expropriation risk and unenforceable contracts | Stability is comparative, not absolute | No |
| Transport | The loss of the cost advantage in carriage | Railways came after the first wave | No |

## Railways by country type, in one table

| Setting | Effect | Social result | Caution |
|---|---|---|---|
| Early industrialiser | Completes the transport revolution; integrates a national market | Urban growth, commuting, engineering demand | Railways followed mechanisation |
| Continental catch-up | Often state-promoted; national economic integration | A market unified before a state, in the German case | Timing and state role varied |
| Settler frontier | Opens interior land to settlement and commercial agriculture | A bound continental market - and indigenous dispossession | Say the dispossession if social effects are asked |
| Late, state-assisted | Instruments of state modernisation and strategic reach | Compressed modernisation with heavier social strain | Not spontaneous market outcomes |
| Colonial | Producing districts to ports; administration and troops inland | Deepened export dependence, not a diversified home market | Indian evidence belongs to its own owner |

## Britain and Japan, in one table

| Axis | Britain | Japan |
|---|---|---|
| Initiating agent | Private enterprise | The state, from 1868 |
| Motive | Profit and market opportunity | National security |
| Political precondition | A commercially favourable state | Unification and centralisation first |
| Technology | Domestically generated, 1764-1785 | Imported and adapted |
| Leading sector | Textiles | Cotton and silk |
| Infrastructure | Private and incremental; railway 1830 | A government programme from 1868 |
| Institutions | Reactive: 1802, 1824, franchise reform | Deliberate: constitution 1889, Diet 1890 |
| Labour response | Legalised combination, then a constitutional demand | Police suppression of a workers' and farmers' party |
| Military link | Present but not defining | Defining: wars of 1894-95 and 1904-5 |
| Vulnerability | Export markets and imported raw cotton | Acute: collapse in mid-1921; export shrinkage after 1929 |

## Argument map: was it a revolution?

```text
  CLAIM: the term "revolution" is justified
      |
      +-- EVIDENCE FOR: irreversible change in the leading sectors, the
      |   source of power, the ownership of the means of production and
      |   the expected role of the state
      |
      +-- OBJECTION: it took a century; sectors changed unevenly;
      |   handwork persisted and in places expanded; agriculture still
      |   employed large numbers
      |
      +-- REPLY: the test is irreversibility of structure, not speed of
      |   diffusion; no industrialising economy returned to a
      |   predominantly domestic-handicraft structure
      |
      +-- RESIDUE: no workforce-composition data is held here, so the
      |   quantitative core of the continuity case cannot be settled
      |
      +-- VERDICT: gradual in diffusion, revolutionary in structure
```

## Argument map: blessing or catastrophe?

```text
  CLAIM: it was a mixed blessing - but asymmetrically
      |
      +-- OPTIMIST EVIDENCE: output rose, goods became cheaper,
      |   long-run prosperity expanded
      |
      +-- PESSIMIST EVIDENCE: long hours, low wages, women's and
      |   children's labour, slums, structural insecurity
      |
      +-- THE SOURCE'S OWN POSITION: industrialisation was a source of
      |   both benefits and hardships, so "mixed blessing" is legitimate
      |   when supported with evidence
      |
      +-- THE SHAPE THAT MAKES IT A VERDICT: gains general and delayed;
      |   costs concentrated and immediate
      |
      +-- WHY IT EXPLAINS THE POLITICS: those who paid could see the
      |   benefit accruing elsewhere, so they demanded a share -
      |   regulation, combination, the vote - not the end of industry
      |
      +-- RESIDUE: the aggregate question is unsettled and may be
      |   unanswerable on a heterogeneous population
```

## Argument map: industry and empire

```text
  CLAIM: industrial capitalism raised the stakes of empire rather
         than inventing the impulse
      |
      +-- MECHANISM: output -> raw materials, markets, investment
      |   outlets -> competition among industrial powers -> colonial
      |   domination and imperial rivalry
      |
      +-- EVIDENCE: the sources connect industrialisation with the
      |   search for markets and raw materials, and record that
      |   industrial countries subordinated weaker economies
      |
      +-- AGAINST CAUSATION: empires predate industry; what changed was
      |   empire's FUNCTION - colonies as required suppliers and captive
      |   markets rather than trading posts
      |
      +-- AGAINST THE OPPOSITE ERROR: imperialism cannot be explained
      |   only by racism or only by nationalism
      |
      +-- LIMITS: not all colonies were profitable; partition's ranked
      |   drivers belong to the imperialism owner; the India verdict
      |   belongs to the Modern Indian History owner
```

## Answer-writing spine

```text
  1. DEFINE STRUCTURALLY        agrarian and handicraft -> industrial;
                                putting-out -> factory
  2. STATE THE THESIS           conjunction, not cause / capacity outran
                                welfare / magnification / intensification
  3. SUPPLY THE MECHANISM       conditions -> cotton -> steam -> factory
                                -> transport
  4. SUPPLY THE CONSEQUENCE     class, gender, city, health, protest
  5. SUPPLY THE RESPONSE        1802 -> 1824 -> Chartism, with both
                                cautions attached
  6. WIDEN (20 marks only)      diffusion or empire; environment;
                                divergence
  7. QUALIFY                    unevenness by sector, region, craft,
                                country and group
  8. VERDICT                    graded, conceding something specific,
                                answering the exact wording
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. Definition, baseline and the contested word

- Two definitions: ✅ the rapid changes of the later eighteenth century that **revolutionised techniques of production** and created an **industrial economy**; ✅ the transition from an **agrarian and handicraft economy** to one dominated by **industry and machine manufacturing**.
- ✅ Linked in the sources to three things together - **capitalism**, the **factory system** and **steam power**.
- ✅ The **factory system replaced the domestic or putting-out system**: merchant supplies materials, household works with its own tools at its own pace, payment by the piece; the merchant controls commerce but not production.
- ⚠️ The factory is therefore a solution to a **supervision problem**, not a larger workshop.
- ✅ Write it as **both technological and social**. ✅ Production shift: domestic/putting-out to factory. ✅ Social shift: rural-to-urban migration, industrial bourgeoisie, wage proletariat.
- ✅ Pace objection recorded: use "revolution" for **impact**, not for **uniform pace**; ✅ industrialisation was a **long, uneven process**.
- ⚠️ The test that saves the word: **irreversibility of structure**, not speed.

## 2. Chronology and the dating controls

- ✅ 1764 spinning jenny (Hargreaves) · 1769 water frame (Arkwright) and the era of Watt's breakthrough · 1785 power loom (Cartwright) · 1793 cotton gin (Eli Whitney, **United States**) · 1802 First Factory Act · 1824 anti-union laws repealed · 1830 first railway for passengers · 1830s-40s Chartism.
- ✅ Start conventions: **c. 1750 onward in England**; **c. 1760s-1840s** for a span. ✅ Never a single year.
- ⚠️ Universal safe phrasing: "from about the middle of the eighteenth century".
- ✅ Watt **improved** rather than solely invented the steam engine.
- ⚠️ 1830 is used as the source's own convention for the first passenger railway; no other railway date is asserted.
- ✅ No British output, national income, wage or population figures exist in this evidence base.
- ⚠️ Enclosure is taught as a mechanism and deliberately left **undated**.
- ⚠️ The lag argument: 38 years from first machine to first Act; 22 more to lawful combination; then the franchise demand.

## 3. Why Britain: the conjunction and its six mechanisms

- ⚠️ Thesis: **not any single advantage** but capital, coal, cheap displaced labour, agricultural surplus, transport and secure commercial law **simultaneously available** - a conjunction, not a cause.
- ⚠️ Three tests for every named condition: necessary? sufficient? what mechanism?
- ✅ **Agriculture** - crop rotation, manuring, new implements; more food and labour for towns. Limit: regionally uneven.
- ✅ **Enclosure** - created landless labour; mechanism: workers with **no alternative subsistence** who therefore **accepted factory time-discipline**. Limit: long and contested.
- ✅ **Coal** - removed the **ceiling water power imposed on mill siting and scale**. Limit: resources alone explain nothing.
- ✅ **Iron** - with coal, supported machinery production; machine-building expands. ⚠️ Recursive: machines make iron cheaper, cheaper iron makes machines.
- ✅ **Capital** - from expanding trade, colonies and plundered wealth; made fixed investment financeable **before returns arrived**. Limit: colonial profit is one source among several.
- ✅ **Markets** - overseas trade and colonies widened demand; justified producing **far beyond local need**. Limit: domestic demand also mattered.
- ✅ **Institutions** - a post-seventeenth-century state favourable to commerce; **lowered expropriation risk and enforced contracts**. Limit: stability is comparative.
- ✅ **Transport** - canals, macadam roads, steam boats, later railways; lowered delivered cost so the **advantage survived the journey**. Limit: railways followed the first wave.
- ✅ Ranking: remove one element and the sequence slows; remove **secure markets and cheap coal together** and it does not begin.
- ⚠️ The circularity is broken at the first round: the initial market and the initial capital came from the pre-existing commercial and colonial economy.

## 4. Cotton, steam and the factory - the industrial mechanism

- ⚠️ Why textiles led: universal price-sensitive demand; repetitive decomposable work; an existing merchant organisation with capital and distribution.
- ⚠️ The bottleneck chase: jenny and water frame push the constraint to weaving; the power loom pushes it back to raw fibre; the gin clears it.
- ⚠️ 1764-1785 imbalance: cheap machine yarn **expanded** hand weaving before the power loom removed it - displacement was delayed, then abrupt.
- ⚠️ The gin's US origin makes the industry's **intercontinental raw-material dependence** structural.
- ✅ Steam: production **leaves the limits of muscle and water power**; removes the **siting and scale limit**; unit costs keep falling.
- ⚠️ Power ceases to be a place and becomes a purchase; four constraints removed - site, scale, season, site scarcity; mills cluster, and clustering makes towns.
- ⚠️ The loop: coal to steam to more coal and iron to better machines including better engines.
- ✅ Steam did not cause everything: capital, labour, resources, agriculture and markets also mattered.
- ✅ **Factory system replaced putting-out**; **standardised supervised production raised output per worker beyond artisan capacity**.
- ⚠️ Four transfers of control: tool, pace, quality and material, place - all in the same direction.
- ⚠️ Four sources of the productivity gain: the machine, continuity, specialisation, supervision - the last two obtained by reducing the worker's independence.
- ⚠️ Five specific losses: tool ownership, control of hours, breadth of skill, unity of home and work, security.
- ✅ Landless, toolless workers depended on employers; **economic insecurity becomes structural**.
- ⚠️ Task-time gives way to clock-time; hours become countable, therefore legislatable.

## 5. Transport and the differentiated railway

- ✅ Recorded together: railways, canals, macadam roads, steam boats; raw materials and finished goods move faster.
- ⚠️ The rule: railways **magnified whatever economic order they entered**.
- ✅ Britain - completes the transport revolution; national market; pulls forward coal, iron, engineering; **followed** mechanisation.
- ✅ Continental catch-up - growth after 1815; often state-promoted; national economic integration; ⚠️ in Germany a market unified before a state.
- ✅ Boundary: the political narrative of Italian and German unification belongs to the unification owner, and nationalism as a doctrine to the political-philosophies owner; this topic supplies only the economic integration mechanism.
- ✅ Settler frontier - growth especially after 1870; interior land opened to settlement and commercial agriculture; ⚠️ indigenous dispossession must be stated.
- ✅ Russia and Japan - later and state-assisted; instruments of state modernisation and strategic reach; compressed modernisation carried heavier strain.
- ⚠️ Colonies - lines from producing districts to ports, and inland for administration and troops; export dependence deepened.
- ⚠️ Common effects: urbanisation and commuting; nationally standardised time; cheaper long-distance migration; famine **relief and exposure**; new occupational groups and early transport unionism; social mixing in carriages.

## 6. The industrial society: towns, classes, capitalism

- ✅ Urbanisation - economic life shifts from village to city; industrial towns dominate modern life. ✅ Caution: concentrated in specific industrial regions.
- ✅ New classes - capitalists and wage-workers become central; class politics deepens.
- ✅ **Industrial capitalism**: means of production **concentrated in private hands**; a new class society.
- ⚠️ Class here is a **relation to the means of production**, not an income band.
- ✅ Slum conditions in early phases; **public health, housing and sanitation become state problems for the first time**.
- ⚠️ The mill-town inversion: workplace first, population immediately after, arrangements last - so the crisis was **unassignable**.
- ✅ **Laissez-faire** = the early capitalist belief in minimum state interference; ✅ recorded as one of at least four historical forms of capitalism, and this period supplies it.
- ✅ Boundary: doctrines of capitalism, socialism, communism, social democracy, trade unionism and the welfare state belong to the political-philosophies owner.

## 7. Women, children and the household

- ✅ Long hours, low wages, women and child labour; ✅ women's and children's labour was **central** to the early factory regime.
- ✅ Industrialisation **restructured the household economy**, not just the workplace.
- ✅ Caution: women's factory work **coexisted with continued domestic and outwork labour** - no single trajectory.
- ⚠️ Four reasons the labour was usable: the machine supplies force; narrow repetitive tasks; attendance over craft judgement; supplementary earners paid less.
- ⚠️ Household consequences: production leaves the home; income becomes a sum of individually vulnerable wages; **care and work become incompatible in time and place**; old domestic labour continues.
- ⚠️ Child labour was regulated in 1802, twenty-two years before combination was legalised, because a child cannot consent, the harm was visible and attributable, and regulation extended protection while combination transferred power.

## 8. The standard-of-living argument

- ✅ Three axes: living standards; state against market; speed of change - each resolved by a **distinction**, not a winner.
- ✅ The narrative account itself ends by calling industrialisation a source of both **benefits and hardships or evils**; ✅ "mixed blessing" is legitimate **with evidence**.
- ⚠️ Core formulation: productive capacity rose **faster than welfare**, and the gap produced the modern social question.
- ⚠️ Shaped verdict: gains **general and delayed**; costs **concentrated and immediate**.
- ⚠️ Graded verdict: a decisive gain in long-run productive power purchased at a heavy, unequally distributed short-run social cost.
- ⚠️ Four reasons an aggregate fails: heterogeneous population; group-specific timing; money income is not welfare; regional concentration.
- ⚠️ No wage, price, height, mortality or consumption data is held here.

## 9. The response and the erosion of laissez-faire

- ✅ 1802 First Factory Act - the state begins regulating labour and hours; **laissez-faire weakens**.
- ✅ 1824 repeal of anti-union laws - **workers forced legal recognition over time**; collective bargaining enters modern politics.
- ✅ 1830s-40s Chartism - workers demand **political rights, not only wages**; the social question becomes a **constitutional** question.
- ✅ Cautions: early Acts were **weakly enforced**; Chartism **did not achieve its immediate aims**.
- ⚠️ Why each rung failed: endurance cannot solve a structural problem; protection defined by others is revocable; organisation governed by others is containable.
- ⚠️ Laissez-faire was eroded **from inside** by harms the industrial order itself produced.
- ✅ Trap: industrialisation did **not** mean free trade everywhere; many late industrialisers used **protection**.

## 10. Diffusion, unevenness and the later surge

- ✅ England earliest and most advantageous start; France, Belgium, Switzerland after 1815; Germany later but powerful; the United States especially after 1870; Russia and Japan later and state-assisted.
- ✅ Trap: it began in England and spread **unevenly**; never claim even European diffusion.
- ✅ The United States had a **vast and successful industrial revolution mainly in the last quarter of the nineteenth century**, and by **1914 had surpassed Britain and Germany in coal, iron and steel output**.
- ✅ American prosperity was **not shared equally**: immigrants, black Americans and women often faced low wages and poor conditions; **labour unions and the Socialist Party** formed; big business was unsympathetic and these had little success before 1914-18.
- ✅ Over **30 million** people arrived in the United States from abroad between **1860 and 1930**.
- ⚠️ Organising axis: **who initiated and under what compulsion** - market advantage, national integration, state security.
- ⚠️ Unevenness proves no condition was sufficient and that industrialisation was not an automatic stage.
- ⚠️ First wave: cotton, steam, coal, iron, factory. Later surge: measured on heavier metrics; no sectoral account is held here and the numbered label is not used by the sources.
- ✅ Boundary: the Fourth Industrial Revolution appears in a verified 2020 General Studies Paper II question routed to a governance owner.
- ✅ Boundary: national integration is an economic mechanism here; the unification narratives and nationalism as a doctrine belong to their own owners.

## 11. The latecomer route - Japan, with Russia as a control

- ✅ 1853 Perry at Yokohama with four battleships; treaties over five years felt as **"a great national humiliation"**; **"gradually a determination to modernize and strengthen the country developed"**.
- ✅ 1868 Meiji restoration - **building railways, improving the road system, starting modern industries such as cotton and silk manufacture**, and a parliamentary system **modelled on Germany's constitution**; Japan becomes a **unified and centralized empire** for the first time in over two and a half centuries.
- ✅ Motive stated as security: preventing the Western powers from treating Japan as they had treated China.
- ✅ 1889 constitution formally accepted; 1890 first Diet; house of appointed peers, cabinet appointed by the emperor, Privy Council; the system was **"far from democratic"**.
- ✅ Wars with China 1894-95 and Russia 1904-5, both won; the Russian defeat was **the first time an Asian country had defeated a European great power**.
- ✅ First World War: cotton-cloth exports **almost trebled**; merchant fleet **doubled in tonnage**.
- ✅ The boom ends **mid-1921**; farmers and industrial workers attempting to form a political party were **"ruthlessly suppressed by the police"**; politicians **"regularly accepted bribes from big business"**.
- ✅ 1929 crisis: exports **shrink disastrously**; tariffs raised against Japanese goods; raw silk **below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932**.
- ⚠️ Organising contrast: Britain industrialised because private actors found it profitable; **Japan because the state decided it was necessary**.
- ⚠️ Latecomer advantage: import proven technology, skip invention. Latecomer cost: compete with a cheaper incumbent; depend on external demand.
- ✅ Controls: no named combines, houses, dates or shares; no model-enterprise sale; no land-tax reform; no dates or figures beyond those listed.
- ⚠️ Russia in **one sentence**: state-led industrialisation is a family of cases whose common risk is social strain outrunning political adaptation.
- ⚠️ Verdict: **faster, not superior**.

## 12. Empire, environment and the divided world

- ✅ Chain: output -> raw materials, markets, investment outlets -> competition among industrial powers -> colonial domination and imperial rivalry.
- ✅ Industrial countries **subordinated the economies of weaker regions** to their own interests.
- ✅ Defensible formulation: industry did **not mechanically create empire** but **greatly intensified the pressure** for it.
- ✅ Trap: imperialism cannot be explained only by racism or only by nationalism.
- ⚠️ The sharper claim: industrial capitalism **raised the stakes** of empire, converting colonies into required suppliers and captive markets.
- ✅ Environment: coal-based production concentrated **smoke, waterway pollution and occupational disease** in industrial districts; keep it **qualitative**.
- ⚠️ The environmental costs behaved like the sanitary crisis - concentrated, imposed, unassignable - and became public questions.
- 📰 Single current linkage: the United Kingdom's last coal-fired power station, **Ratcliffe-on-Soar**, ceased coal generation on **30 September 2024**, making the country that began coal-fired industry the first G7 economy to phase out coal power; the world's first coal-fired station was **Holborn Viaduct, London, 1882**.
- ✅ Global effect: **uneven industrialisation, imperial demand for markets and raw materials, and an international division of labour**.
- ⚠️ No divergence measurement is available; teach the mechanism, refuse the number.

## 13. The India hand-off

- ✅ Five-step English mechanism: textile mechanisation 1764-1793; steam removing the siting and scale limit; the factory system; transport lowering delivered cost; structural pressure outward.
- ✅ Hand-off sentence: English mechanisation, steam and cheap transport made machine-made cloth structurally cheaper than hand production; **whether, how far and where** that produced handicraft decline depends on colonial policy, patronage and demand conditions inside India.
- ✅ Boundary rule, non-negotiable: the "how far" verdict - tariff asymmetry, lost court and aristocratic patronage, revenue policy, transport penetration, regional and craft-wise variation, and the scholarly dispute over extent - belongs to the **Modern Indian History** owner.
- ✅ Cheaper competing supply is a **pressure, not an automatic outcome**; decline was **uneven by craft, region and market segment**.
- ✅ English mechanisation mattered **through colonial tariff, market and power structures** - not as a technology-only explanation.

## 14. The four debates

- ✅ **Revolution?** Rupture against continuity; settled by separating **impact from pace**.
- ✅ **Blessing?** Optimist against pessimist; settled by separating **short-run misery from long-run productivity gain**, then stating distribution.
- ⚠️ **Cause?** Five single-cause claims, five distinct failure modes; settled by the **sufficiency test** and the removal ranking.
- ✅ **State or market?** Settled by showing **why labour laws and the modern state grew**.
- ✅ Empire contributed markets and resources but **does not alone explain Britain's take-off**.
- ⚠️ Attribution limit: this evidence base records positions **without naming historians**; none is named here.
- ⚠️ Template: name the axis, state both sides, supply the distinction, take a qualified position, state the residue.
- ⚠️ Open questions: the quantitative living-standards answer; the relative weight of the six conditions; the domestic-against-colonial apportionment of initial capital and demand.

## 15. Answer architecture

- ✅ Syllabus clause names the **industrial revolution** and asks for **"their forms and effect on the society"** - an instruction to analyse consequences.
- ✅ Directive map: why England first (ranking and interaction, not an invention list) · social consequences (class, gender, childhood, city, health, protest) · blessing or curse (short-run cost against long-run gain, graded) · railways (differentiate by country type) · how far responsible outside England (mechanism, then hand over) · industry and imperialism (intensification, not causation) · latecomer (comparison route) · why the state began regulating (the social question forcing law).
- ✅ Thesis templates: conjunction not cause · capacity outran welfare · gain purchased at concentrated cost · stakes not impulse · industry as an instrument of national security.
- ✅ Mark scaling: **10** thesis, two mechanisms, one consequence, one qualification, verdict · **15** thesis, four to five conditions, mechanism chain, consequences, state and labour response, qualification, verdict · **20** plus diffusion or the global dimension, environment and demography, historiographical caution, exact-wording verdict.
- ✅ Bank selection: railways for one technology in several economies; Britain-Japan for different routes into industrialisation.
- ✅ Verdict scaffolds: mixed blessing asymmetrically · a conjunction, not a cause · railways magnify · industry raised the stakes.

## 17. Prohibited formulations

- Any single start year for the Industrial Revolution.
- "Watt invented the steam engine."
- Any British output, national income, wage, urban-share or population figure.
- "The cotton gin was invented in Britain."
- "The Industrial Revolution spread evenly across Europe."
- "Industry caused empire" - and equally, "empire had nothing to do with economics".
- "The Industrial Revolution destroyed Indian handicrafts."
- Any Indian deindustrialisation statistic, artisan estimate or drain figure taken from this topic.
- Any named Japanese business combine, house, founding date or ownership share as sourced fact.
- "The Meiji state founded model factories and sold them to private groups", or any land-tax financing claim.
- Any Japanese figure beyond the three recorded, or any Japanese date beyond 1853, 1868, 1889, 1890, 1894-95 and 1904-5.
- "Industrialisation meant free trade everywhere."
- Any emission, mortality, real-wage, height or divergence statistic.
- Any named historian or school attached to the optimist, pessimist, rupture, continuity or causation positions.
- Any railway date other than the 1830 convention, or any claim about which line was first beyond that convention.
- Any provision, clause or enforcement detail of the Factory Acts, or any specific Chartist demand.

| 2 | Nine dated markers with functions; the two start conventions; four dating and attribution controls; the 38-22 lag ladder; why chronology substitutes for statistics here | Annotated chronology spine; marker table; convention table; lag ladder | 7 | Building a reactive-state argument from four dates | Feeds Q5 |
| 3 | Necessary, sufficient and conjunctural conditions; the three tests; the agricultural mechanism; enclosure as availability and as willingness; demography within the evidence; the first two sufficiency tests | Logic-only decision panel; agricultural derivation flow; three-test table | 7 | Pre-empting the "others had it too" objection | Feeds Q2 |

## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. Its complete text was extracted directly from the local PDF and searched. It supplied, from its Japan chapter: the arrival of Commodore Matthew Perry at Yokohama with four battleships in 1853 and the demand that Japan open trade; the treaties of the following five years and their reception as "a great national humiliation"; the statement that "gradually a determination to modernize and strengthen the country developed"; the 1868 restoration of the Meiji emperor and the policy of "building railways, improving the road system, starting modem industries, like cotton and silk manufacture" with a parliamentary system "modelled on Germany's constitution"; the statement that Japan became "a unified and centralized empire" for the first time in over two and a half centuries; the security motive for occupying neighbouring territories; the wars with China in 1894-95 and Russia in 1904-5 and the observation that the Russian defeat was "the first time that an Asian country had defeated one of the European great powers"; the 1889 constitution, the house of appointed peers, the cabinet, the Privy Council, the first Diet of 1890 and the statement that the system "was far from democratic"; the First World War trade boom in which "the exports of Japanese cotton cloth almost trebled, while their merchant fleet doubled in tonnage"; the end of the boom in "the middle of 1921"; the suppression of the attempted farmers' and workers' party, which was "ruthlessly suppressed by the police"; the record that "many politicians were corrupt and regularly accepted bribes from big business"; and the 1929 crisis in which "exports shrank disastrously", tariffs were raised against Japanese goods and by 1932 the price of raw silk "had fallen to less than one-fifth of the 1923 figure". It also supplied, from its chapter on the United States before the Second World War: the "vast and successful industrial revolution, mainly in the last quarter of the nineteenth century"; the statement that by 1914 the United States "had easily surpassed Britain and Germany, the leading industrial nations of Europe, in output of coal, iron and steel"; the record that prosperity "was not shared equally", with immigrants, black Americans and women facing low wages and poor living and working conditions, leading to the formation of labour unions and the Socialist Party against unsympathetic big business with little success before 1914-18; and the figure of "over 30 million" arrivals from abroad between 1860 and 1930.
2. **Control result for the British core.** The same extracted text was searched for every central term of this topic. The searches returned **zero** occurrences of "spinning jenny", "power loom", "steam engine", "factory system", "enclosure", "Chartism", "Hargreaves", "Cartwright" and "cotton gin", and the single occurrence of "industrial revolution" falls in the American chapter quoted above. This confirms the folder README's statement that the book begins in 1914 and is structurally insufficient for Topics 01-08, and the book is therefore **not cited anywhere in this session for the British material**. The zero-return is recorded as evidence of a check performed, not as a source used.
3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.
4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above or from the canonical owners. No contemporary observer, reformer, parliamentarian, economist or historian is quoted, because no such quotation is held in this evidence base. The book's own optical rendering prints "modem industries" for "modern industries"; the slip is recorded here and the corrected reading is used in teaching.

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning sessions and solved workbooks and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates this topic.

- No statute is named beyond "the First Factory Act" and "the Factory Acts", and no provision, age limit, hour limit, inspection arrangement or enforcement detail is asserted. No union, strike, reformer, minister or Chartist demand is named, because none is held here.
- No enclosure statute, date or extent is asserted; the mechanism is taught and the chronology is left open, as Lesson 2 records.
- The 1830 first-passenger-railway marker is used strictly as the source's own convention. Its scope is stated inside Lesson 2, no competing date or line is asserted, and the point is recorded here as open uncertainty rather than silently resolved.

No Meiji model-enterprise sale and no land-tax reform is asserted. No Japanese date or figure appears beyond those verified in the core book and listed in the register notes.
- No historian, school label or work is named in connection with the rupture, continuity, optimist, pessimist or causation positions, because the evidence base records those positions without attributions. The limitation is stated openly in Lesson 16 rather than concealed.
- The doctrines of capitalism, socialism, communism, social democracy, nationalism, trade unionism and the welfare state are routed to the political-philosophies owner; the drivers and partition of New Imperialism are routed to the imperialism owner; the political narrative of Italian and German unification is routed to the unification owner on the basis of the folder's own topic map, which was read, and no part of that owner's material was consulted or reproduced; and none of these bodies of material appears here.
- Objective-question neutrality does not arise, because this topic owns no objective question; the Mains questions displayed carry directive, demand and answer approach without a solved model answer, as the governing boundary requires.
- Remaining open uncertainty, recorded rather than filled: the quantitative living-standards question; the relative weight of the six British conditions beyond the single recorded ranking; the apportionment between domestic and colonial contributions to initial capital and initial demand; the pace of the steam-for-water transition; the proportion of the workforce actually in factories at any date; the financing of the Meiji programme; and the institutional organisation of Japanese business.
